Competition Creates Competition: Stock Prices and the
           Discovery of Takeover Bidders
                                            Austin Li


                                              Abstract
      A stronger incumbent bidder can attract a challenger when it makes the target’s stock price
  more informative. I study a takeover auction in which a prospective buyer sees the price before it
  pays to prepare a bid. A stronger incumbent lowers the buyer’s acquisition profit at every belief
  about its quality. It also makes target proceeds more sensitive to that quality, so an investor
  who knows the buyer’s quality class gains more from trade. The investor shares one order
  opportunity with liquidity traders, so no order is conclusive.
      On an open set of parameters, a stronger incumbent turns an uninformative price into an
  informative one, with unique trading and preparation outcomes. Challenger entry rises, and so
  does the probability that a challenger from the high-value class acquires the target. With the
  information in the price held fixed, entry does not rise. Between the two economies, informative
  and uninformative equilibria coexist, and entry rises weakly with incumbent strength along the
  informative branch. When the investor pays for research, research and entry rise together over a
  range of strengths. In the strong-incumbent economy, access to prices raises expected target
  proceeds and acquisition surplus net of preparation costs.




                                                  1
1    Introduction
At fixed information, a stronger incumbent reduces a prospective challenger’s expected return from
entry into a takeover contest. The challenger must pay preparation costs before it can submit an
executable offer, and a stronger rival lowers what that preparation earns (Fishman, 1988; Hirshleifer
and Png, 1989). I study a force in the opposite direction. Competition can make the target’s stock
more informative about the challenger. When a potential buyer observes that price before it commits
to acquisition preparation, the information effect can outweigh the loss of acquisition profits. A
stronger incumbent then attracts another buyer and raises the probability that a challenger from the
high-value class acquires the target.
    Consider a listed target with a public sale opportunity. One buyer is already prepared, and another
has not committed to the diligence that an executable proposal requires. Section 2.1 describes the
decision interval in which that buyer decides while the target’s stock trades. Incumbent strength is
the public distribution of the prepared bidder’s value, and a stronger incumbent shifts it upward. No
offer is announced before the challenger decides.
    The mechanism runs through two claims on the same acquisition surplus. The challenger holds
the acquisition claim: what it keeps if it prepares, bids, and wins. Target shareholders hold the
traded claim: what the cash second-price auction pays them. The challenger’s value comes from a
high-value or a low-value quality class, and the classes fix the expected value gap between them. A
stronger incumbent outbids the challenger more often and then pays the challenger’s bid, so more of
the gap reaches target proceeds. A challenger that wins pays more, so less of the gap remains as
acquisition profit at every belief.
    Target shares therefore become more sensitive to information about the challenger, while the
challenger’s own return to preparation falls. An outside specialist investor who knows the challenger’s
class trades on that sensitivity. It holds no net position in the target. Target shareholders gain when
the price recruits entry, because a holder through the sale receives sale proceeds, not the interim
price.
    The trading stage is one order slot, with the alternative-arrival structure of Dow and Gorton
(1997). With some probability the informed investor fills it. Otherwise a liquidity trader fills it with
a sale, a purchase, or no order, and no one observes who placed the order. A liquidity order in the
same direction is always possible, so no order reveals the class. The posterior after every order stays
inside fixed bounds under every investor strategy, and the investor keeps a residual advantage after
every order. A larger gap in target proceeds can make that advantage worth the cost of trade.
    The main result compares a weak-incumbent economy with a strong-incumbent economy on a
nonempty open set of primitives. In each economy the trading outcome and the on-path preparation
outcome are unique. With the weak incumbent, the investor’s residual advantage cannot cover the


                                                   2
trading cost, the price reveals nothing, and only a zero-cost challenger prepares. With the strong
incumbent, the investor buys after good news and sells after bad news, and a purchase brings in
a high-cost challenger. Entry rises even though acquisition profit falls at every fixed belief. The
probability that a challenger from the high-value class owns the target rises with it.
    Market makers anticipate the preparation that each price induces, so the reversal holds under
rational pricing. Uniqueness covers every mixed order profile on the menu {−1, 0, 1} and every
unilateral deviation. The posterior bounds hold for every profile, and they deliver this uniqueness.
The change in information does the work. If the investor’s orders stay fixed at their informative level
while the incumbent strengthens, entry does not rise (Section 4.2).
    In the benchmark of Section 4.2, entry rises from 0.250000 at the weak incumbent to 0.522757
at the strong incumbent. High-class ownership rises from 0.125000 to 0.324401. Over the same
change, gross acquisition profit at the prior falls from 4.804167 to 4.291667, and the target-payoff
spread rises from 0.016667 to 0.666667. Against a strong enough incumbent, even the most favorable
price cannot justify expensive preparation. Entry then returns to its floor while the price stays
informative, so entry is non-monotone in strength. In the benchmark, high-cost preparation stops
above the strength 𝑟𝐶 ≈ 3.592659.
    The hypotheses of the main result are strict inequalities, so they define an open set of primitives.
At the benchmark auction primitives, they hold exactly when 82/59 < 𝑢 < 2686/1559. Here 𝑢 is
the informed presence mass relative to the liquidity mass in one direction. The number 1 + 𝑢 is
the largest likelihood ratio that one order can carry. Equivalently, the top posterior at the prior 1/2
lies between the preparation thresholds of the strong and the very strong incumbent, 141/200 and
4245/5804. Section 4.2 reports this region and the range of priors as a table.
    Section 4.3 gives the complete equilibrium set between and beyond the benchmark strengths.
Informative and uninformative equilibria coexist on an interval of strengths [𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝑁 ], with 𝑟 𝐴 ≈
1.393958 and 𝑟 𝑁 ≈ 1.747878. On the informative branch, the high-class investor first mixes between
a purchase and abstention, and entry rises strictly with strength. From the strength 𝑟 𝐹 ≈ 1.467127,
the investor buys for sure, and entry stays flat on the rest of the interval. Entry therefore rises weakly
along the informative branch and strictly where the investor mixes. The same start of coexistence
holds for a window of priors around the benchmark.
    Section 5 lets the investor pay for research that makes it informed. The investor chooses the
probability of success up to a cap, at a quadratic cost. The unique outcome has no research with
the weak incumbent and research at the cap with the strong incumbent. At intermediate strengths,
research and entry rise together. At the strengths 1.55, 1.60 and 1.65, research rises from 0.237901
through 0.279037 to 0.316683, and entry from 0.507630 through 0.513965 to 0.519763. Along
this branch the comovement holds for every liquidity probability of the model, and an equilibrium
without research exists beside it.


                                                    3
    The reversal holds at some incumbent strengths and costs for every prior and every pair of
classes that differ above the reserve. It needs the high class to have stochastically higher values and
both class supports to lie above the reserve (Section 6.2). With more than two classes, it holds
under a separation condition. The rise of entry survives other laws of preparation costs, with or
without atoms (Section 6.1). It also survives private signals that the buyer and the investor hold
about different facts (Section 6.3). In an example with its own costs and strengths, the buyer’s signal
is more accurate than the investor’s, and the rise needs both signals.
    In the strong-incumbent economy, access to prices raises expected target proceeds and acquisition
surplus net of preparation costs (Section 7.1). Sale terms shape both what a buyer pays when it wins
and what the stock price can reveal before anyone prepares. A reserve that excludes the low-value
class makes the price informative against both incumbents, and entry is then flat in incumbent
strength (Section 7.3). An alternative bargaining institution shows how the payment rule matters
(Section 7.2). The effect of competition on the target-payoff spread depends on the weight the rule
places on the runner-up’s value.
    The model also gives tests (Section 8). The purchase impact exceeds the sale impact exactly
when a purchase recruits the buyer. In the benchmark, this entry component is 90.2% of the spread
between purchase and sale prices. Against a strong prepared bidder, orders no longer predict entry,
but they still predict which bidder wins. Given the entry floor and the target-payoff spread, the sale
impact and the frequency of orders identify the informed share.
    The paper belongs to the literature on the real effects of financial markets (Bond et al., 2012).
The closest antecedent is Dow et al. (2017), where a firm’s investment decision feeds back into
the incentive to produce information about its stock. Here the sale rule divides acquisition surplus
between a traded claim and a buyer that decides whether to enter. Edmans et al. (2015) show that
corrective real decisions can discourage trade on bad news under rational pricing. Here the real
decision is the buyer’s entry itself.
    Firms learn from peers’ stock prices (Foucault and Frésard, 2014), and target prices affect
takeover activity (Edmans et al., 2012). In this model, a prospective challenger learns about its own
acquisition value from the target’s price before it commits. Bidders also learn from announcement
returns when they decide whether to complete a deal (Luo, 2005). That decision comes after this
entry margin.
    The auction-entry literature makes the bidder pool endogenous (Samuelson, 1985; McAfee and
McMillan, 1987; Levin and Smith, 1994; Gentry and Stroup, 2019). It also shows how selective
entry affects the choice of sale procedure (Roberts and Sweeting, 2013). The challenger here faces
selective entry in the terms of that literature. Its pre-entry information indexes the distribution of its
value, and the payment of the entry cost reveals the value (Roberts and Sweeting, 2013). One limit
of this framework is a bidder that knows only the common value distribution before entry (Levin


                                                    4
and Smith, 1994). The other limit is a bidder that already knows its value (Samuelson, 1985).
     Here the pre-entry information is the target’s stock price. That price is public, and it moves with
the orders of an investor who observes the challenger’s quality class. I keep the direct deterrence
force of the auction-entry literature and add a market that operates before preparation. Auction
formats also affect bidders’ incentives to acquire information (Persico, 2000). The informed trader
here is outside the auction and trades a payoff that differs from the entrant’s profit.
     Recent work studies how bidders learn about their own values and about competitors (Pernoud
and Gleyze, 2026). It also studies post-auction feedback in security-payment design (Liu and
Bernhardt, 2022) and bidder-pool choice with correlated values (Carlin et al., 2026). In takeovers,
Betton et al. (2014) study negotiations with stock-market feedback, and Lin et al. (2025) model
payment choice and trade within an initiated deal. I hold cash consideration fixed and study entry.
     Arbitrageurs’ positions can affect tendering (Cornelli and Li, 2002). The investor here has
information about a prospective acquirer and no role in tendering. The model abstracts from toeholds
and dispersed-shareholder free riding (Bulow et al., 1999; Grossman and Hart, 1980). Section 5
makes the investor’s information the result of costly research, with the alternative-arrival structure
of Dow and Gorton (1997). Goldstein and Guembel (2008) study a related channel, manipulation
through the real decision that responds to the price.
     An informed trader can also hold a stake before a control decision. Liquidity trade then lets an
acquirer conceal share purchases before a bid (Kyle and Vila, 1991). Large holders trade on their
information (Edmans, 2009), monitor, or intervene as prices feed back to firm decisions (Edmans et
al., 2015, Section II.E; Maug, 1998; Back et al., 2018). The investor in this paper holds no stake,
seeks no control, and earns only the trading residual.
     Section 2 describes the decision interval and sets out the model. Section 3 derives the two
returns to information and what the challenger learns from the price. Section 4 states the main
result, the benchmark with its fixed-information control, and the coexistence of equilibria. Section 5
adds costly research. Section 6 covers robustness, Section 7 welfare and sale terms, and Section 8
empirical implications, and Section 9 concludes. Appendix A proves every result and states the
results of Sections 6 to 8 in full.


2     The model

2.1    The decision interval
The model applies to a listed target whose sale opportunity is publicly understood while its stock
still trades. In this interval, a further bidder can still come in. A disclosed approach, an announced
strategic review, or an open contest can create such an interval. The opportunity must become public


                                                   5
before the buyer set is fixed. Only then can a prospective buyer watch the price during the period
that matters. Whether a given transaction fits is a question about its chronology, and Section 8 states
how to date it.
     Within the interval, one buyer is already prepared. It may be the party that approached the
target or a bidder that has completed diligence. Its strength is what the public can infer about its
acquisition value from the disclosed record. Another buyer has not decided whether to prepare.
Preparation is required for an executable proposal.
     Before a buyer can submit a binding offer, it must verify the target’s contracts, liabilities, and
technology. It must also arrange financing and approvals and commit evaluation capacity that has
other uses. Those costs are sunk once incurred, and they differ across buyers. Prior familiarity,
capacity already in place, or a small deal relative to the buyer’s size make preparation cheap for
some buyers and expensive for others. A buyer that does not prepare cannot bid its current expected
value instead; it stays out. The challenger’s cost is an entry cost, not the price of an optional report.
     The investor and the buyer know different things. A buyer knows its own integration plans. A
specialist investor may know what the target’s customers do, how its technology compares with
alternatives, or how its product demand will change. These are different facts about the same
acquisition match, so a more accurate signal about one can leave the other unknown.
     The model gives the investor exact knowledge of the challenger’s quality class, though not of
its value, and suppresses the buyer’s initial information. Section 6.3 removes both extremes, and
Section 5 makes the investor’s information the result of costly research. The identity of the further
buyer is common knowledge; what the market does not know is how well that buyer fits the target.
     Public incumbent strength is a distribution, not a bid. The record can reveal that a strategic
acquirer with clear synergies is at the table. It can also reveal that the prepared party is a financial
buyer with a leveraged capital structure. In each case it need not reveal what either buyer would
pay. Strategic and financial bidders differ systematically in their valuations (Gorbenko and Malenko,
2014). The model varies that distribution and keeps the incumbent’s realized value private until it
bids.
     Disclosure records document a costly preparation stage. Imprivata’s definitive proxy separates
an unsolicited approach, outreach to potential buyers, and indications of interest conditional on
further diligence (Imprivata, Inc., 2016). Takeover competition develops before public bids (Boone
and Mulherin, 2007; Gentry and Stroup, 2019).


2.2    Values, preparation, and the sale
The target’s known standalone value is normalized to zero. Acquisition values and preparation costs
are measured per target share. A common standalone component added to every ownership outcome


                                                   6
shifts prices and payoffs by a constant and leaves incentives unchanged.
    All strategic agents are risk neutral. The target share is the only traded claim with information
about the acquisition match, and neither bidder trades it. The investor has no control rights and cannot
acquire the target. The sale mechanism binds all target shares, so shareholder tendering and holdout
stay outside the model. The incumbent’s value distribution is public when the sale opportunity
becomes visible. Its realized value stays undisclosed to the challenger before preparation.
    Two potential acquirers face the target. The incumbent has already prepared, incurs no further
entry cost, and knows its value before it bids. Its acquisition value 𝑅 has a continuous distribution 𝐹
on [0, 𝑟],
        ¯ conditional on public information when the sale process begins. The distribution 𝐹 is
extended by unity above 𝑟.¯ A first-order stochastic shift of 𝐹 strengthens the incumbent. In the main
case 𝑅 is uniform on [0, 𝑟], and an increase in 𝑟 strengthens the incumbent.
    The challenger belongs to one of two quality classes, 𝑗 ∈ {𝐻, 𝐿}, with prior 𝜋 = Pr( 𝑗 = 𝐻) ∈
(0, 1). Given its class, its acquisition value 𝜃 has a distribution 𝐺 𝑗 on [0, ∞) with a finite mean;
atoms are allowed. Write 𝐺 𝑗 ( 𝑝 − ) for the probability that a class- 𝑗 value falls below the reserve 𝑝,
and 𝐷 (𝑦) = 𝐺 𝐿 (𝑦) − 𝐺 𝐻 (𝑦). Two restrictions rank the classes.

Assumption 1 (Class ranking). The high class, or high-value class, dominates the low class in the
sense of first-order stochastic dominance. Both class supports lie above the reserve, so that every
value meets it:

                    𝐺 𝐻 (𝑦) ≤ 𝐺 𝐿 (𝑦)   for every 𝑦,      𝐺 𝐻 ( 𝑝 − ) = 𝐺 𝐿 ( 𝑝 − ) = 0.

    Under Assumption 1, competition widens the class difference in target proceeds under every
incumbent distribution (Proposition 1). Appendix A.1 characterizes the weakest restriction of this
kind. When neither class has a value atom at the reserve, the spread rises under every strengthening
exactly when two conditions hold. The high class dominates above the reserve, and the two classes
are equally likely to fall below it, which I call equal admissibility. Section 7.3 studies a reserve that
excludes the low class, where equal admissibility fails.
    The structure is the selective-entry structure of the auction literature. The class indexes the
distribution of the challenger’s value, preparation reveals the value, and the stock price is the
pre-entry signal.
    The benchmark is the point-mass case: 𝐺 𝐻 puts all its mass on ℎ, 𝐺 𝐿 puts all its mass on ℓ, and
𝜋 = 1/2. In the benchmark the class and the value coincide. Values, the reserve, and the uniform
incumbent’s strength satisfy
                                          0 < 𝑝 < ℓ < 𝑟 < ℎ.                                          (1)

    The general statements need no normalization of money. A common positive rescale of every
value, the reserve, and every preparation, trading and research cost rescales prices and payoffs by

                                                   7
the same factor. Orders, beliefs, entry, and ownership do not change. The benchmark measures
money in units of the low value, so ℓ = 1 there.
    The challenger initially knows neither its class nor its value. After it observes the stock price, it
privately learns its preparation cost 𝐶 ∈ {0, 𝑐 𝐻 }, with Pr(𝐶 = 0) = 𝜌, 0 < 𝜌 < 1, and 𝑐 𝐻 > 0. The
low cost is zero: a zero-cost challenger is a ready buyer whose diligence costs nothing at the margin.
A payment of 𝐶 reveals 𝜃 and permits a bid. A challenger that does not pay stays outside the sale.
    The pair ( 𝑗, 𝜃), the incumbent’s value, the preparation cost, and the draws of the order slot in
Section 2.3 are mutually independent. The cost therefore carries no information about the class or
the value. Conditional on the class, the challenger’s value is independent of the order.
    Before trade, the seller publicly commits to a cash second-price auction with reserve 𝑝 > 0.
The highest admissible bidder acquires the target. It pays the larger of the reserve and the highest
competing admissible bid. A bid equal to the reserve is admissible, and there is no sale without
an admissible bid. Each prepared bidder whose value meets the reserve bids its value. This bid is
weakly dominant (Appendix A.1).
    A prepared challenger whose value is below the reserve does not bid. Without a challenger, the
incumbent buys at the reserve when its value meets it. Expected no-entry proceeds are therefore
𝑡0 = 𝑝 [1 − 𝐹 ( 𝑝)]: the board collects its announced walk-away price from a lone prepared bidder
whose value meets it. Because 𝐹 is continuous, acquisition-value ties have probability zero.


2.3    Trading: the order slot
An investor observes the challenger’s class 𝑗. It is an outside sector specialist, such as a sector-focused
long-short fund. It holds no net position in the target before trade, and a holder with a full hedge
counts as an outsider in this sense. Its information concerns the challenger’s fit with the target. It
forms that view from its own research on the target’s customers, technology, and demand. Neither
bidder nor the target supplies this information.
   Trade takes place in one order slot:

  (1) One public order opportunity comes before the challenger’s decision.

  (2) With probability 𝜆 ∈ (0, 1), the informed investor fills the slot. The investor sees the class 𝑗
      and chooses an order 𝑞 ∈ {−1, 0, 1}. A nonzero order costs 𝑘 > 0.

  (3) With probability 1 − 𝜆, a liquidity trader fills the slot. It sells, abstains, or buys one unit with
      probabilities 𝛽, 1 − 2𝛽, and 𝛽, where 0 < 𝛽 ≤ 1/3. Its motive is exogenous.

  (4) No one observes who filled the slot. The identity draw is independent of the class, the costs,
      and the incumbent’s value.


                                                    8
  (5) Competitive market makers see the order 𝑋 ∈ {−1, 0, 1} and set the price 𝑃 = E[𝑉𝑇 | 𝑋].
      The challenger sees the price and then decides whether to prepare.

Write 𝜂(−1) = 𝜂(1) = 𝛽 and 𝜂(0) = 1 − 2𝛽 for the liquidity order distribution.
   If the investor fills the slot with order 𝑞, its profit is

                                         𝑞 [𝑉𝑇 − 𝑃(𝑞)] − 𝑘 |𝑞|,                                         (2)

where 𝑉𝑇 is the terminal payoff of a target share. The investor’s order is the whole order that market
makers see, so it trades at the price 𝑃(𝑞). The unit is a normalized small trading unit, not ownership
of the target. The cost 𝑘 is a separate friction, such as an execution cost or a cost to carry the position.
It is distinct from adverse-selection price impact, which the competitive price already generates.
The investor’s order after bad news is a short sale, and 𝑘 includes the cost to borrow shares.
     Liquidity traders fill the slot when the investor does not. A current shareholder sells one unit
for cash, an outside investor buys one unit, or the opportunity passes with no order. Each acts for
reasons apart from the sale. Competitive market makers absorb the order at the price 𝑃(𝑋). Each
share held at the sale, market-maker inventory included, receives 𝑉𝑇 .
     Two numbers summarize the slot. The probability of a liquidity order in a given direction is
𝑏 = (1 − 𝜆) 𝛽. The ratio 𝑢 = 𝜆/𝑏 is the informed presence mass relative to the liquidity mass in one
direction. The number 1 + 𝑢 is the largest likelihood ratio that one order can carry. Section 3.2
shows that, under every investor strategy, the posterior after every order lies in [𝑚(𝜋), 𝑀 (𝜋)], where

                                  𝜋(1 + 𝑢)                                𝜋
                    𝑀 (𝜋) =                    ,         𝑚(𝜋) =                      .                  (3)
                              𝜋(1 + 𝑢) + 1 − 𝜋                    𝜋 + (1 − 𝜋)(1 + 𝑢)

     The bounds follow from one property of the slot. Each class reaches each order through a
liquidity trader, so no order is conclusive about the class. The investor therefore keeps a residual
advantage after every order.
     The slot uses the alternative-arrival structure of Dow and Gorton (1997): an informed order
and a liquidity order of the same size are alternative arrivals in one opportunity. In Admati and
Pfleiderer (2009), a liquidity shock forces the informed holder’s own sale, so a sale occurs on path
for both motives. The unit order menu, the presence probability, and the symmetric unit cost follow
Edmans et al. (2015). Risk-neutral market makers with zero expected profit price each order, as
in Glosten and Milgrom (1985) and Kyle (1985). Here the terminal payoff depends on the price
through preparation.
     Market makers observe the order 𝑋 and nothing else. The challenger observes the price 𝑃 and
its own cost 𝐶, but not 𝑋, its class, its value or the incumbent’s value. The investor observes the
class 𝑗 but not the value 𝜃, and the incumbent knows its own value. The sequence is as follows:


                                                     9
  (1) The seller announces the sale rule.

  (2) The investor learns the class. The slot draw selects the investor with probability 𝜆 and a
      liquidity trader otherwise, and the selected trader places its order.

  (3) Market makers set the price from the order.

  (4) The challenger observes the price and its cost and decides whether to prepare.

  (5) Prepared bidders learn their values and submit their bids.

  (6) Ownership and financial payoffs are realized.

Section 5 adds a research stage before step (2).


2.4    Equilibrium
For a known challenger value 𝜃, write 𝑡 (𝜃) for expected target proceeds after the challenger prepares
and 𝑔(𝜃) for the challenger’s expected gross acquisition profit. As before, 𝑡 0 is expected target
proceeds without entry. The class objects are expectations over the class distribution,

                        𝑡 𝑗 = E 𝑗 [𝑡 (𝜃)],    𝑔 𝑗 = E 𝑗 [𝑔(𝜃)],       𝑗 ∈ {𝐻, 𝐿},

and the target-payoff spread is Δ𝑇 = 𝑡 𝐻 − 𝑡 𝐿 . Bids are truthful, so these objects depend on the
incumbent’s distribution and the reserve but not on any belief.
   At a belief 𝜇 = Pr( 𝑗 = 𝐻), the challenger’s expected gross profit is

                                        𝐵(𝜇) = 𝑔 𝐿 + 𝜇 (𝑔𝐻 − 𝑔 𝐿 ),

because its value depends on the price only through its class. When the incumbent’s distribution
matters, I write 𝑔 𝑗 (𝐹), Δ𝑇 (𝐹), and 𝐵 𝐹 . For the uniform incumbent on [0, 𝑟], I write 𝑔 𝑗 (𝑟), Δ𝑇 (𝑟),
and 𝐵𝑟 .
    An equilibrium is an explicit object. It lists the investor’s orders, the beliefs, the prices, and the
entry rule. Each condition below can then be checked against the object by direct computation.

Definition 1 (Equilibrium). An equilibrium is a tuple
                                           (︁                 )︁
                                       E = 𝜎𝐻 , 𝜎𝐿 , 𝜇, 𝑃, 𝜇,

together with the bids of the prepared bidders. Its components are as follows.

           (︁                        )︁

     informed investor in class 𝑗 ∈ {𝐻, 𝐿}. Mixed and wrong-signed orders are allowed.

   • 𝜇 : {−1, 0, 1} → [0, 1] is the market makers’ posterior 𝜇(𝑥) = Pr( 𝑗 = 𝐻 | 𝑋 = 𝑥).

   • 𝑃 : {−1, 0, 1} → R is the price after each order.

   • 𝜇¯ assigns to each price 𝑃¯ in the image of 𝑃 the challenger’s belief Pr( 𝑗 = 𝐻 | 𝑃(𝑋) = 𝑃).

         ¯ ∈ [0, 1] is the probability that a challenger with the high cost prepares after the price 𝑃.

     A challenger with the zero cost prepares after every price.

For each order 𝑥, write 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) = (1 − 𝜆)𝜂(𝑥) + 𝜆𝜎 𝑗 (𝑥) for its probability in class 𝑗, and 𝑒(𝑥) =
𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌) 𝜁 (𝑃(𝑥)) for entry after it. The tuple satisfies six conditions.

  (E1) Bayes consistency. For every order 𝑥, 𝜇(𝑥) = 𝜋 𝑓𝐻 (𝑥)/[𝜋 𝑓𝐻 (𝑥) + (1 − 𝜋) 𝑓 𝐿 (𝑥)].

       over 𝑞 ∈ {−1, 0, 1}, with 𝑃(·) and 𝑒(·) held at their values in E.

    The gross profit of a zero-cost challenger is an expected auction rent, which is never negative.
Preparation is therefore optimal for it after every price, and the definition fixes this choice. The
probability 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) is at least (1 − 𝜆)𝜂(𝑥) > 0 under every strategy. Every order therefore occurs with
positive probability in both classes. Bayes’ rule defines 𝜇 after every order, and no off-path belief
enters the definition.
    Condition (E5) uses the expected terminal payoff of a target share, given class 𝑗 and order 𝑞.
Entry depends only on the price and the cost, and the cost is independent of the class and the order.
That payoff is therefore 𝑡 0 + 𝑒(𝑞)(𝑡 𝑗 − 𝑡 0 ). The same independence and (E1) turn (E4) into
                                                [︁                  ]︁
                               𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑡 0 + 𝑒(𝑥) 𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡 0 + Δ𝑇 𝜇(𝑥) .                              (4)

                                                  11
The ex ante payoff of the investor multiplies 𝑈 𝑗 by 𝜆. This factor leaves the best order unchanged.
     Two indifferences remain: an investor can be indifferent among orders, and a high-cost challenger
can be indifferent about preparation. The equilibrium concept lets an indifferent investor mix over
its best orders in any proportion. This is the standard best-response condition, and I call it rule IM.
An indifferent high-cost challenger prepares, and I call this rule C1. Two alternatives show which
conclusions depend on these choices:

    • under rule I0, an investor indifferent between abstention and a nonzero order abstains;

    • under rule CM, an indifferent high-cost challenger may prepare with any probability.

The results use IM and C1. A remark states each place where I0 or CM changes a conclusion.
    Two comparisons recur. A unilateral deviation by the investor changes only its own order. The
price function 𝑃 and the entry rule 𝜁 stay at their values in the candidate equilibrium. The deviation
payoff is therefore 𝑈 𝑗 (𝑞) read at the candidate’s 𝑃(𝑞) and 𝑒(𝑞). A comparison across economies,
such as across incumbent strengths, solves for the equilibrium again in each economy. Proofs use
the first comparison to test a candidate equilibrium and the second to compare outcomes.
    Two outcomes summarize an equilibrium. Entry E is the probability that the challenger prepares.
High-class ownership O𝐻 is the probability that a challenger of class 𝐻 acquires the target. Write
              [︁               ]︁
𝑊𝐻 (𝐹) = E𝐻 𝐹 (𝜃)1{𝜃 ≥ 𝑝} for the probability that a prepared challenger of class 𝐻 wins against
the incumbent distribution 𝐹.


3     Competition and information

3.1    Acquisition profits and target value
The auction determines both the return to preparation and the claim that informed investors trade.
Consider a prepared challenger with value 𝜃 ≥ 𝑝. If it wins, it pays max{𝑝, 𝑅}; if it loses, the
incumbent pays 𝜃. The target therefore receives max{𝑝, min(𝑅, 𝜃)}, and the challenger keeps
(𝜃 − max{𝑝, 𝑅})+ . A prepared challenger with 𝜃 < 𝑝 cannot bid. The target then receives the
reserve if the incumbent’s value meets it, exactly as without entry.
    For every value at or above the reserve, the two expected amounts add up to the value:

                                    𝑡 (𝜃) + 𝑔(𝜃) = 𝜃,      𝜃 ≥ 𝑝.

The second-price rule passes the whole value of an admissible challenger to the target and the
challenger together. An admissible value also raises proceeds to at least the reserve, so 𝑡 𝑗 ≥ 𝑡0 for
both classes.

    In the point-mass benchmark with the uniform incumbent, integration over 𝑅 gives

   Thus a stronger incumbent lowers acquisition profit at every fixed belief. At the same time, it
makes target proceeds more sensitive to challenger quality. Proposition 1 extends this opposition to
every continuous incumbent distribution and every pair of classes that satisfies Assumption 1.

Let 𝐹˜ ≤ 𝐹 be a continuous first-order stochastic strengthening of 𝐹.

 (a) For every pair of classes, the strengthening weakly decreases 𝑔𝐻 and 𝑔 𝐿 , and hence gross

 (b) Under Assumption 1, the strengthening weakly increases the target-payoff spread.

    The proof works one incumbent value at a time. At a value 𝑅 that meets the reserve, the target
                                                                                   ∫𝑅
receives min{𝑅, max{𝜃, 𝑝}}. The expected class difference in proceeds is then 𝑝 𝐷 (𝑦) 𝑑𝑦. Read
the integrand as slices 𝐷 (𝑦) 𝑑𝑦 of the class value gap at each level 𝑦 > 𝑝. The slice at 𝑦 reaches the
target when the incumbent outbids the challenger at that level and pays its bid. It stays with the
challenger otherwise.


                                                       13
    A stronger incumbent moves slices to the target. The spread rises and acquisition profit falls.
The reserve adds one term. Below the reserve, only an admissible challenger buys, at 𝑝, and the
class difference is 𝑝𝐷 ( 𝑝 − ). A stronger incumbent fails the reserve less often, so that term shrinks.
It can outweigh the moved slices when the low class is more often inadmissible.
    Under Assumption 1, every value meets the reserve, and the term is zero. Appendix A.1 gives
the proof. It also gives, in Proposition 1(c), the weakest restriction under which the spread rises for
every strengthening.
    The sum of the two lines of (7) under Assumption 1 is

                  Δ𝑇 (𝐹) + 𝑔𝐻 (𝐹) − 𝑔 𝐿 (𝐹) = E𝐻 [max{𝜃, 𝑝}] − E 𝐿 [max{𝜃, 𝑝}].

The identity holds for every incumbent distribution. In the benchmark, the right side is ℎ − ℓ at
every 𝑟. A stronger incumbent leaves the value gap between the classes unchanged. It moves the
gap from the challenger’s profit to target proceeds.
    The spread approaches zero as 𝑟 approaches ℓ, since the incumbent then almost never outbids a
low-value challenger. As 𝑟 increases, the spread rises while gross acquisition profit falls at each
belief. Whether entry rises depends on how this change in payoffs affects the information in the
price.


3.2    What the challenger learns from the price
The challenger sees one price. This section states what that price can reveal under every investor
strategy, and what it reveals in equilibrium. Write 𝛿 𝑞 for the point mass at the order 𝑞, and
𝑏 0 = (1 − 𝜆)(1 − 2𝛽) for the probability of a liquidity abstention. Under any order distributions
𝜎𝐻 , 𝜎𝐿 , Bayes’ rule in odds form reads

                                       𝜇(𝑥)     𝜋    𝑓𝐻 (𝑥)
                                             =     ·        .                                       (8)
                                     1 − 𝜇(𝑥) 1 − 𝜋 𝑓 𝐿 (𝑥)

Lemma 1 (Order probabilities and beliefs; analytical). Fix any order distributions 𝜎𝐻 and 𝜎𝐿 .

(a) Full support. 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) ≥ (1 − 𝜆)𝜂(𝑥) > 0 for every order 𝑥 and class 𝑗.

(b) Nonzero orders. For 𝑥 ∈ {−1, 1}, 𝑏 ≤ 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) ≤ 𝑏 + 𝜆. Hence 𝑚(𝜋) ≤ 𝜇(𝑥) ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋).

 (c) The zero order. 𝑏 0 ≤ 𝑓 𝑗 (0) ≤ 𝑏 0 + 𝜆, and 𝑏 0 ≥ 𝑏. Hence 𝑚(𝜋) ≤ 𝜇(0) ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋).

(d) Full orders. If 𝜎𝐻 = 𝛿1 and 𝜎𝐿 = 𝛿−1 , the posteriors after the orders (−1, 0, 1) are
    (︁               )︁
       𝑚(𝜋), 𝜋, 𝑀 (𝜋) .



                                                  14
    Each class reaches an order in a given direction through a liquidity order with probability
𝑏, and the informed investor adds at most 𝜆. The likelihood ratio 𝑓𝐻 (𝑥)/ 𝑓 𝐿 (𝑥) therefore lies in
[1/(1 + 𝑢), 1 + 𝑢], and by (8) the odds of 𝜇(𝑥) lie between the odds of 𝑚(𝜋) and those of 𝑀 (𝜋).
At the zero order the liquidity mass is 𝑏 0 , and 𝑏 0 ≥ 𝑏 is the inequality 𝛽 ≤ 1/3. With 𝛽 > 1/3, a
profile in which class 𝐻 abstains and class 𝐿 never abstains gives 𝜇(0) > 𝑀 (𝜋). Appendix A.2
gives the proofs of this section.
    Part (c) is the only use of the bound 𝛽 ≤ 1/3, and Corollary 1 uses it. Propositions 2 and 3 and
Corollary 2 use parts (a), (b) and (d) only, so they hold for every 𝛽 < 1/2.
    The challenger’s belief at a price is an average of the posteriors of the orders with that price, so
it obeys the same bounds. A preparation decision that needs a belief above 𝑀 (𝜋) therefore follows
no price.

Corollary 1 (High-cost preparation under every profile; analytical). Let 𝑔𝐻 ≥ 𝑔 𝐿 . If 𝐵(𝑀 (𝜋)) < 𝑐 𝐻 ,
then no profile that satisfies (E1)–(E3), in or out of equilibrium, has high-cost preparation after any
order.

    Corollary 1 is the ceiling of the model. Against a strong enough incumbent, gross profit falls
below the high cost even at the top posterior, and no order can recruit an expensive challenger.
    When 𝑔𝐻 > 𝑔 𝐿 , write
                                                 𝑐𝐻 − 𝑔𝐿
                                           𝜏=
                                                 𝑔𝐻 − 𝑔 𝐿
for the preparation threshold. Gross profit covers the high cost exactly when the belief reaches the
threshold: 𝐵(𝜇) ≥ 𝑐 𝐻 if and only if 𝜇 ≥ 𝜏. For the uniform incumbent I write 𝜏(𝑟).

Lemma 2 (Entry and prices; analytical). Let Δ𝑇 > 0 and 𝑔𝐻 > 𝑔 𝐿 . Fix a tuple that satisfies
(E1)–(E4) and (E6).

 (a) Floor. 𝑒(𝑥) ≥ 𝜌 for every order 𝑥.
                            [︁                  ]︁
 (b) Price. 𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑡0 + 𝑒(𝑥) 𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡 0 + Δ𝑇 𝜇(𝑥) for every order 𝑥.

 (c) Sufficiency. If 𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑃(𝑥 ′), then 𝜇(𝑥) = 𝜇(𝑥 ′). The challenger’s belief at the price 𝑃(𝑥) is
     𝜇(𝑥).

 (d) Entry. 𝑒(𝑥) = 𝜌 if 𝜇(𝑥) < 𝜏, and 𝑒(𝑥) = 1 if 𝜇(𝑥) ≥ 𝜏.

 (e) Monotonicity. If 𝜇(𝑥) > 𝜇(𝑥 ′), then 𝑒(𝑥) ≥ 𝑒(𝑥 ′) and 𝑃(𝑥) − 𝑃(𝑥 ′) ≥ 𝜌Δ𝑇 [𝜇(𝑥) − 𝜇(𝑥 ′)] > 0.

   Part (c) is the step from orders to prices. Suppose that two orders have one price. The challenger
cannot tell them apart, so entry after them is the same, and it is at least 𝜌. The price formula then
gives the same posterior after both orders. The challenger can therefore read the market makers’

                                                  15
posterior from the price, and its gross profit at the price 𝑃(𝑥) is 𝐵(𝜇(𝑥)). Under rule CM, part (d)
at 𝜇(𝑥) = 𝜏 allows any 𝑒(𝑥) ∈ [𝜌, 1]; the other parts hold under both challenger rules.
    The zero-cost challenger does three jobs in the proofs. Entry stays at least 𝜌, so the price
reveals the posterior. The investor’s return after every order stays at least a fixed share of the spread
(Lemma 3 below). Zero-cost preparation does not respond to the price, so information can only add
entry.


3.3    Informed trading
Rational pricing removes the anticipated increase in proceeds from the investor’s advantage. The
next lemma states what remains, under every candidate tuple.

Lemma 3 (Informed trading; analytical). Let Δ𝑇 ≥ 0. Fix a tuple that satisfies (E1) and (E4), with
entry 𝑒(𝑥) ∈ [𝜌, 1] after every order. Conditional on its arrival, the investor in each class earns

   𝑈𝐻 (1) = 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(1)] − 𝑘,         𝑈𝐻 (0) = 0,      𝑈𝐻 (−1) = −𝑒(−1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(−1)] − 𝑘,
  𝑈 𝐿 (−1) = 𝑒(−1)Δ𝑇 𝜇(−1) − 𝑘,            𝑈 𝐿 (0) = 0,       𝑈 𝐿 (1) = −𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 𝜇(1) − 𝑘.

Write 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(1)] and 𝐾 𝐿 = 𝑒(−1)Δ𝑇 𝜇(−1) for the gross returns of the two correct
orders.

 (a) A wrong-signed order earns at most −𝑘, so abstention strictly beats it.

 (b) Class 𝐻 buys if 𝐾 𝐻 > 𝑘 and abstains if 𝐾 𝐻 < 𝑘. At 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑘, the purchase and abstention are
     its two best orders. Class 𝐿 sells if 𝐾 𝐿 > 𝑘 and abstains if 𝐾 𝐿 < 𝑘. At 𝐾 𝐿 = 𝑘, the sale and
     abstention are its two best orders.

 (c) Under every candidate profile, 𝜌Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝑀 (𝜋)] ≤ 𝐾 𝐻 ≤ Δ𝑇 and 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) ≤ 𝐾 𝐿 ≤ Δ𝑇 . Hence
     𝜌 𝜅(𝜋)Δ𝑇 ≤ 𝐾 𝑗 ≤ Δ𝑇 , where 𝜅(𝜋) = min{𝑚(𝜋), 1 − 𝑀 (𝜋)}.

 (d) 𝐾 𝐻 depends on the candidate only through 𝜇(1) and 𝑒(1), and 𝐾 𝐿 only through 𝜇(−1) and
     𝑒(−1).

    The investor’s gross return is the entry probability times the spread times the market’s residual
uncertainty about the class. The market makers price the entry response, but a liquidity order in
the same direction is always possible, so some uncertainty survives every order. The lower bound
in part (c) uses the entry floor 𝜌 and the posterior bounds of Lemma 1. The upper bound is the
spread. Both bounds hold under every candidate profile, mixed or pure, and their comparison with
𝑘 decides when information survives in equilibrium.


                                                   16
    The bound 𝜅(𝜋) has a simple form. Since 1 − 𝑀 (𝜋) = 𝑚(1 − 𝜋), 𝜅(𝜋) = 𝑚(min{𝜋, 1 − 𝜋}). It
equals 𝑚(1/2) = 1/(2 + 𝑢) at the prior 1/2 and is smaller at every other prior. The class that the
prior favors binds, because the price already leans toward it.
    The no-entry proceeds 𝑡0 cancel from each return, because they move the price and the terminal
payoff together. The terms that matter are the entry gains 𝑡 𝐻 − 𝑡0 and 𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡0 , which are nonnegative.
    Part (d) splits the investor’s problem. Entry after the zero order enters no payoff. Each class
therefore compares one gross return with the trading cost: class 𝐻 the return of a purchase, class 𝐿
the return of a sale. Section 4.3 uses this split to list every equilibrium along the benchmark path.


4     Equilibrium results

4.1    Competition creates competition
The main result compares three economies that differ only in incumbent strength. In the first, the
price carries no information and only a zero-cost challenger prepares. In the second, the price is
informative and a purchase recruits a high-cost challenger. In the third, the price stays informative
and entry returns to its floor. Write 𝐹0 , 𝐹1 and 𝐹2 for the three incumbent distributions, 𝐵𝑖 = 𝐵 𝐹𝑖 ,
Δ𝑖 = Δ𝑇 (𝐹𝑖 ), 𝑀 = 𝑀 (𝜋), 𝑚 = 𝑚(𝜋) and 𝜅 = 𝜅(𝜋).

Proposition 2 (Competition creates competition; analytical). Let the classes satisfy Assumption 1.
Let 𝐹1 be a continuous first-order stochastic strengthening of the continuous incumbent distribution
𝐹0 , and let 𝐹2 be one of 𝐹1 . Fix 0 < 𝜋 < 1, 0 < 𝜌 < 1, 𝑘 > 0 and a slot (𝜆, 𝛽). Suppose

                                  (H1)       𝐵0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 < 𝐵1 (𝑀),
                                  (H2)       Δ0 < 𝑘 < 𝜌 𝜅 Δ1 ,
                                  (H3)       𝐵2 (𝑀) < 𝑐 𝐻 .

Then each economy has a unique equilibrium outcome.

 (i) Weak incumbent 𝐹0 . The investor does not trade: 𝜎𝐻 = 𝜎𝐿 = 𝛿0 . Every posterior equals 𝜋,
     entry equals 𝜌 after every order, and every order has the price 𝑡0 + 𝜌 [𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡 0 + Δ0 𝜋]. The
     price carries no information.

(ii) Strong incumbent 𝐹1 . The investor trades in both directions: 𝜎𝐻 = 𝛿1 and 𝜎𝐿 = 𝛿−1 . The
     posteriors after the orders (−1, 0, 1) are (𝑚, 𝜋, 𝑀). Only a purchase recruits the high-cost
     challenger, so entry after the three orders is (𝜌, 𝜌, 1), and 𝑃(−1) < 𝑃(0) < 𝑃(1). Entry is

                                      E1 = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜋𝜆) > 𝜌.

                                                  17
     Entry given class 𝐻 is 𝑒 𝐻 = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜆). High-class ownership rises from 𝜋𝜌𝑊𝐻 (𝐹0 )
     to 𝜋𝑒 𝐻 𝑊𝐻 (𝐹1 ) if and only if

                                 [𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜆)] 𝑊𝐻 (𝐹1 ) > 𝜌 𝑊𝐻 (𝐹0 ).


(iii) Very strong incumbent 𝐹2 . The investor again trades in both directions, with posteriors
      (𝑚, 𝜋, 𝑀). Entry equals 𝜌 after every order. The three prices differ, so the price stays
      informative while entry returns to 𝜌.

    Uniqueness covers every mixed order profile on the menu {−1, 0, 1} and every unilateral
deviation. Every investor comparison in the proof is strict, and every equilibrium posterior lies
strictly on one side of the threshold. No tie rule is therefore used, and the same unique outcome
holds under all four pairs of tie rules. Appendix A.3 states the three equilibrium tuples and checks
each condition of Definition 1 against them.
    The hypotheses fix trading before any order profile is known. By Lemma 3, every correct order
earns at most Δ0 − 𝑘 < 0 in the weak economy and at least 𝜌𝜅Δ1 − 𝑘 > 0 in the strong one. The
two sides of (H1) set entry. Gross profit at the prior falls short of the high cost at 𝐹0 , and gross
profit at the top posterior exceeds it at 𝐹1 . Hypothesis (H3) stops recruitment at 𝐹2 (Corollary 1).
Appendix A.3 shows that the proof uses each hypothesis once and that (H1) cannot be weakened to
𝐵1 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 .
    The trading window in (H2) is nonempty exactly when Δ0 /Δ1 < 𝜌𝜅. This condition involves
the two incumbent distributions, the classes, 𝜌, the slot and 𝜋, and no unit of money. A prior away
from one half narrows the window, since 𝜅(𝜋) falls. For uniform incumbents, (H1)–(H3) are strict
inequalities between continuous functions of (𝑟 0 , 𝑟 1 , 𝑟 2 , 𝑐 𝐻 , 𝑘, 𝜌, 𝜋, 𝜆, 𝛽). They define an open set.
Section 4.2 gives its slice through the benchmark.
    The additional entry favors the high class: class 𝐻 places a purchase with probability 𝑏 + 𝜆,
class 𝐿 with probability 𝑏. Ownership needs its own condition when the high class can lose to the
incumbent, because a stronger incumbent then lowers 𝑊𝐻 . The condition in part (ii) holds whenever
(1 + 𝜌)𝑊𝐻 (𝐹1 ) ≥ 2𝜌𝑊𝐻 (𝐹0 ) > 0 and 𝑏 + 𝜆 > 1/2. In the point-mass benchmark 𝑊𝐻 = 1, so
ownership rises.
    Part (iii) completes a rise and a later fall of entry with incumbent strength. Against the very
strong incumbent, even the top posterior fails to justify high-cost preparation, while trading stays
informative. Section 4.3 describes the strengths between the three economies.

4.2      The benchmark and the fixed-information control
The benchmark, which I call 𝐵, has point-mass classes ℎ = 10 and ℓ = 1 and the prior 𝜋 = 1/2. The
reserve is 𝑝 = 1/2, the zero-cost probability is 𝜌 = 1/4, the high cost is 𝑐 𝐻 = 6, and the trading cost
is 𝑘 = 1/50. The incumbent is uniform on [0, 𝑟], at the strengths 𝑟 0 = 6/5, 𝑟 1 = 3 and 𝑟 2 = 18/5.
Money is measured in units of the low value.
     The slot has two primitives. The largest likelihood ratio of an order is 1 + 𝑢 = e, where e
is Euler’s number; I print it upright to separate it from entry 𝑒(𝑥). The liquidity probability is
𝛽 = 0.294655, an exact decimal. The presence probability follows: 𝜆 = 𝑢𝛽/(1 + 𝑢𝛽) = 0.336122,
and 𝑏 = 0.195615. Every computation keeps 𝜆 unrounded, and every statement that depends on e
uses interval arithmetic. The posterior bounds at the prior are 𝑀 (1/2) = e/(1 + e) = 0.731059 and
𝑚(1/2) = 0.268941. Appendix A.9 lists these declarations.
     Table 1 reports the acquisition payoffs. From 𝑟 0 to 𝑟 1 , gross acquisition profit at the prior falls
from 4.804167 to 4.291667, while the spread rises from 0.016667 to 0.666667. The benchmark
satisfies (H1)–(H3), so Proposition 2 applies. Entry rises from 0.250000 to 0.522757, and high-class
ownership rises from 0.125000 to 0.324401. At 𝑟 2 , entry returns to 0.250000, while the three prices
still differ. Table 3 lists the three equilibrium tuples.
     The admissible region is a result. Proposition A.1 in Appendix A.3 gives it exactly at the
benchmark auction. At the prior 1/2, (H1)–(H3) hold if and only if 82/59 < 𝑢 < 2686/1559.
Equivalently, the top posterior lies between the thresholds 𝜏(𝑟 1 ) = 141/200 and 𝜏(𝑟 2 ) = 4245/5804.
Outside this band a conclusion of Proposition 2 fails. With 1 + 𝑢 = e, the hypotheses hold exactly
for priors between ≈ 0.467850 and ≈ 0.500424. Table 2 gives the region in decimals, with the
benchmark point and its distance to each boundary.
     The thinnest margins measure one distance. The benchmark top posterior lies 0.000334 below

      Figure 1 shows the threshold along the path. The prior lies below the threshold at every strength,
so an uninformative price never recruits a high-cost challenger. The threshold rises with strength
and meets the top posterior at 𝑟𝐶 , just below 𝑟 2 . Panel (b) shows the band of Proposition A.1(a) for
the top posterior.
      Two controls show where the reversal comes from. The first holds the information in the
price fixed while the incumbent strengthens. Under rule C1, entry then does not rise, for every
information experiment and every independent cost law (Proposition A.3 in Appendix A.6). A
stronger incumbent lowers gross profit at every belief, so it can only remove a preparation incentive.
Entry falls by the probability that the cost lies between gross profit at the two strengths.
      In the slot, the experiment fixes 𝜆, 𝛽, 𝜋, the cost law and both order distributions. Lemma 2(c)
ensures that the competitive prices reveal the same posterior at each strength.
      Table 3 reports both controls. Panel B holds the full orders fixed. Only the top posterior can
recruit, because the threshold 𝜏(𝑟) exceeds the prior at every strength. Entry is therefore 0.522757
at every strength up to the ceiling 𝑟𝐶 = 3.592659, and 𝜌 above it. From 𝑟 0 to 𝑟 1 the probability in
Proposition A.3 is 0.000000; from 𝑟 1 to 𝑟 2 it is 0.272757. With the information held fixed, entry is
unchanged from 𝑟 0 to 𝑟 1 , and it falls once the ceiling binds. Full orders are not an equilibrium at 𝑟 0 ,
since a purchase earns less than its cost there; this row is a control.
      Panel C hides the price from the challenger. The challenger decides on its prior and prepares only
at zero cost. Entry therefore stays at 0.250000 at both strengths, although the investor trades fully at
𝑟 1 . Together, the two controls locate the reversal. Incumbent strength changes the information that

4.3                          Coexistence
Proposition 2 compares 𝑟 0 with 𝑟 1 . Between them, informative and uninformative equilibria coexist.
This section gives the complete equilibrium set along the benchmark path 𝑟 ∈ [𝑟 0 , 𝑟 2 ] at the prior
1/2, for every slot in the band of Proposition A.1(a). Write 𝑀 = (1 + 𝑢)/(2 + 𝑢) and 𝑚 = 1 − 𝑀.
    Two functions organize the path. The first is 𝐽 (𝑟) = Δ𝑇 (𝑟) [1 − 𝜏(𝑟)], the gross return of a
                                                                                     √
purchase that just reaches the threshold and recruits. The second is 𝔯(𝑑) = ℓ + 𝑑 + 𝑑 2 + 2ℓ𝑑, the
root above ℓ of Δ𝑇 (𝑟) = 𝑑. Six strengths mark the changes in the equilibrium set; 𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝑁 and 𝑟 𝐽 do
not depend on the slot.


                               Strength              Definition             Role
                               𝑟𝐴                    𝐽 (︁(𝑟) = 𝑘    )︁      a purchase that just reaches 𝜏(𝑟) and recruits earns 𝑘
                               𝑟𝐹                    𝔯 𝑘/(1 − 𝑀)            a full purchase that recruits earns Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀) = 𝑘
                               𝑟𝑁                    𝔯(2𝑘/𝜌)                against no informed trade, a unit order earns 𝜌Δ𝑇 /2 = 𝑘
                               𝑟𝐽                    𝜌𝐽(︁ (𝑟) = 𝑘)︁         a purchase that just reaches 𝜏(𝑟) without recruitment earns 𝑘
                               𝑟𝑆                    𝔯 𝑘/(𝜌𝑚)               a full sale earns 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚 = 𝑘
                               𝑟𝐶                    𝜏(𝑟) = 𝑀               above it, no order recruits

    Write 𝑠+ = 𝜆𝜎𝐻 (1) for the probability of an informed purchase and 𝑠− = 𝜆𝜎𝐿 (−1) for that of
an informed sale. No equilibrium uses a wrong-signed order (Lemma 3(a)). Five forms of the
high-class order appear on the path:


       Form                                   Purchase probability 𝑠+            Entry after a purchase
       N, no informed trade                   0                                  𝜌
       R, a mixed purchase that recruits      𝑏 [Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/𝑘 − 2]                   one
       F, a full purchase                     𝜆                                  one up to 𝑟 𝐶 , 𝜌 above
       Q, mixed orders at the entry floor     𝑏 [𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/𝑘 − 2]                  𝜌
       T, the threshold pair                  𝑏 (2𝜏 − 1)/(1 − 𝜏), so 𝜇(1) = 𝜏    𝑘/𝐽 (𝑟)

In each form the low class sells with probability 𝑠− = 0 on [𝑟 0 , 𝑟 𝑁 ], 𝑠− = 𝑏 [𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/𝑘 − 2] on
(𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ), and 𝑠− = 𝜆 on [𝑟 𝑆 , 𝑟 2 ]. Entry after no order and after a sale is 𝜌, and the prices follow from
equation (4). Each form thus defines a complete tuple.

Proposition 3 (The equilibrium set on the benchmark path; computer-assisted). Take the benchmark
auction, the prior 1/2 and any slot in the band of Proposition A.1(a). Then 𝑟 0 < 𝑟 𝐴 < 𝑟 𝐹 < 𝑟 𝑁 <
𝑟 𝐽 < 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 1 < 𝑟𝐶 < 𝑟 2 , and the equilibrium set at each strength is

                Strength         Equilibria

                𝑟0 ≤ 𝑟 < 𝑟 𝐴     N
                𝑟 = 𝑟𝐴           N; T with entry one after a purchase (the left end of R)
                𝑟 𝐴 < 𝑟 < 𝑟𝐹     N; R
                𝑟𝐹 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟𝑁      N; F
                𝑟𝑁 < 𝑟 < 𝑟𝐽      F; Q
                𝑟 𝐽 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟2     F

Entry is 𝜌 in N and Q, E 𝑅 (𝑟) = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌) 𝑏Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/(2𝑘) in R, and E𝐹 = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜆/2) in F
up to 𝑟𝐶 , with E𝐹 = 𝜌 above 𝑟𝐶 .

(a) No informed trade and an informative equilibrium coexist exactly on [𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝑁 ].

(b) On the informative branch that coexists with no trade, entry is 𝜌 + (1− 𝜌) min{𝑏Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/(2𝑘), 𝑏 +
    𝜆/2}. It is continuous, rises strictly on [𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝐹 ], and is constant on [𝑟 𝐹 , 𝑟 𝑁 ]. It keeps this value
    along F up to 𝑟𝐶 and equals 𝜌 above 𝑟𝐶 .

 (c) The equilibrium set is nonempty at every strength.

    The status is computer-assisted because two monotonicity steps rest on exact Sturm counts: 𝐽 and
Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝜏) 2 rise along the path. Every other step is analytical. At the benchmark slot, 𝑟 𝐴 = 1.393958,

                                                      23
𝑟 𝐹 = 1.467127, 𝑟 𝑁 = 1.747878, 𝑟 𝐽 = 1.966097, 𝑟 𝑆 = 2.124148 and 𝑟𝐶 = 3.592659. On R, entry
is 0.454185 at 𝑟 𝐴 , 0.459588 at 1.40, and 0.506112 at 1.45. From 𝑟 𝐹 it stays at 0.522757 until 𝑟𝐶 .
The low class abstains up to 𝑟 𝑁 , mixes on (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ), and sells with probability one from 𝑟 𝑆 .
     Figure 2 shows entry along the path. Against an uninformative price with entry 𝜌, a unit
order earns 𝜌Δ𝑇 /2, which stays below 𝑘 up to 𝑟 𝑁 , so no trade survives until 𝑟 𝑁 . An informative
equilibrium changes the entry response and with it the return to trade, so it can hold at the same
strengths.
     Entry rises strictly along R because the purchase rate adjusts. At 𝑟 𝐴 a purchase that just reaches
the threshold earns exactly 𝑘. Above 𝑟 𝐴 , class 𝐻 mixes, and the purchase posterior stays above the
threshold. The purchase then earns Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(1)] = 𝑘, so a larger spread needs a more informative
purchase. The purchase rate 𝑠+ = 𝑏 [Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/𝑘 − 2] therefore rises with 𝑟, and entry rises with it. At
𝑟 𝐹 the purchase rate reaches 𝜆, and from there entry is flat. Entry therefore rises weakly along the
informative branch, and strictly where the investor mixes.
     Above 𝑟 𝑁 , no trade fails. On (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝐽 ) the investor can also mix in both directions without
recruitment (Q), and from 𝑟 𝐽 on, full purchases are the only high-class choice.
Remark 1 (Other tie rules). Under the challenger rule CM, the threshold pair T is also an equilibrium
on (𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝐽 ]. Its entry falls strictly from E 𝑅 (𝑟 𝐴 ) at 𝑟 𝐴 to 𝜌 at 𝑟 𝐽 ; at the benchmark it is 0.341462,
0.321311 and 0.305208 at 1.55, 1.60 and 1.65. At 𝑟𝐶 , entry after a purchase can then take any value
in [𝜌, 1].
     Under the investor rule I0, the equilibrium set is N on [𝑟 0 , 𝑟 𝐹 ]; N and F on (𝑟 𝐹 , 𝑟 𝑁 ]; empty on
(𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ]; and F on (𝑟 𝑆 , 𝑟 2 ]. On (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ] the low class has no admissible sale rate under I0. Abstention
fails, because a sale earns more than 𝑘. A full sale fails, because it earns at most 𝑘. Under the
investor rule IM, the equilibrium set is nonempty along the whole path. The strict rise of entry along
R also needs IM.
                                                                                            (︁                    )︁
     Coexistence also holds at other priors. For a prior 𝜋, write 𝑟 𝑁 (𝜋) = 𝔯 𝑘/(𝜌 max{𝜋, 1 − 𝜋}) .
When some strength on the path has 𝜏(𝑟) ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋), let 𝑟𝐶 (𝜋) be the largest one. When 𝜏(𝑟 0 ) ≤ 𝜋 ≤
𝜏(𝑟 2 ), let 𝑟 𝜋 solve 𝜏(𝑟 𝜋 ) = 𝜋. Three priors divide the cases: 𝜋 lo                                  hi
                                                                            𝐴 solves 𝑀 (𝜋) = 𝜏(𝑟 𝐴 ); 𝜋 𝐴 = 𝜏(𝑟 𝐴 );
and 𝜋max = 𝜏(𝑟 ∗ ), where 𝑟 ∗ solves 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝜏(𝑟) = 𝑘.

5      Competition creates research
So far the investor is informed with a fixed probability 𝜆. This section lets it choose that probability.
Before it learns the class, the investor chooses a research intensity 𝑧 ∈ [0, 𝜆]  ¯ at the cost (𝑎/2)𝑧2 ,
where 𝑎 > 0 and the cap satisfies 0 < 𝜆¯ < 1. Research succeeds with probability 𝑧, independently of
the class, the costs and the incumbent’s value. A success reveals the class, and the investor then fills
the slot with an order. After a failure, a liquidity trader fills the slot.
    Research is therefore the presence probability of Section 2.3, chosen at a cost. More research
also removes liquidity orders, because the liquidity trader fills the slot only after a failure. Market
makers and the challenger do not observe the research choice; they use a conjectured intensity 𝜆.       ˆ
The prior is 1/2 throughout this section.
    An equilibrium with research is a tuple (𝜆,ˆ 𝜎𝐻 , 𝜎𝐿 , 𝜇, 𝑃, 𝜇,
                                                                 ¯ 𝜁), together with the bids. Conditions
(E1)–(E6) of Definition 1 hold at the intensity 𝜆. Condition (E5) holds also at 𝜆ˆ = 0: a deviation
                                                    ˆ
in research makes a success possible, and the order after it must be optimal. A seventh condition
completes the definition.

    (E7) Research optimality. Hold 𝑃 and 𝜁 at their values in the tuple. Then 𝜆ˆ is the research choice
         of a best response among all joint deviations in research and orders.

prices. Appendix A.5 shows that the best research choice is unique and equals min{𝜆,           ¯ 𝑉/𝑎}.
Condition (E7) therefore reads 𝜆ˆ = min{𝜆,  ¯ 𝑉/𝑎}, and research never has a tie.
     Two functions of the intensity describe the branch on which research responds to competition.

     Against strong incumbents, research sits at the cap. Write 𝑟𝑈 = 𝔯 (𝑘 + 𝑎𝜆)(1+e)/𝜌       ≈ 2.434046.
At every strength above 𝑟𝑈 , a bound on every candidate profile forces full orders and research at the
cap (Appendix A.5). On (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟𝑈 ), the equilibrium set with research is open.
     Figure 3 shows the branch. It starts later than the informative branch of Section 4.3, which starts
at 𝑟 𝐴 = 1.393958. A purchase must now pay for research as well as for trade. The two branches
also adjust in different ways. With a fixed presence probability, the purchase rate adjusts through a
mixture of the high class. With research, the research intensity adjusts, and the high class buys with
probability one after a success.
     The quadratic cost gives a unique research response, so no equilibrium needs indifference over
research. Its marginal cost rises and the research value falls, so they meet once, at 𝜆 𝑅 (𝑟). This root
moves smoothly with 𝑟, so entry inherits a strict slope.
     The research parameters have an admissible region, which Proposition A.2 in Appendix A.5
gives exactly at 𝛽 = 0.294655. The claims of Proposition 4 hold with strict margins exactly
when two conditions hold. The cap lies in the strip (𝜆 𝐸 (𝑟 1 ), 𝜆 𝐸 (𝑟 2 )) ≈ (0.290539, 0.336721),
and the cost lies in a window that depends on the cap. At the benchmark cap, this window is
(0.021469, 0.036763). With 1 + 𝑢 = e, the cap 𝜆¯ = 𝑢𝛽/(1 + 𝑢𝛽) and 𝑎 = 1/40, the claims hold for
every 𝛽 ∈ (0.236157, 1/3].
     The upper end of the cap strip is the tightest hypothesis: the benchmark cap lies 0.000599 below
it in research intensity. This margin corresponds to the posterior margin 0.000334 of Section 4.2;
the two numbers measure one boundary in different coordinates.
Remark 2 (Tie rules with research). Research never has a tie, and Proposition 4 needs no investor
tie rule. Under the challenger rule CM, each intermediate strength has one more equilibrium. In
it, research is 𝜆 𝐸 (𝑟), and the purchase posterior equals the threshold. The indifferent challenger
prepares with the probability that makes this research optimal. Its entry falls across the three
strengths (Table 4). Above 𝑟𝑈 , the whole tuple is unique under C1, and under CM at every strength
except the ceiling 𝑟𝐶 = 3.592659. At 𝑟𝐶 under CM, entry can take any value in [𝜌, 0.522757].

prior, the challenger’s information, and the order menu. Appendix A.6 states each result in full and
proves it.

                                                                                                   30
6.1    Preparation costs
The two-point cost law of Section 2.2 is not needed. Let the cost mix a low component 𝐻 0 on
[0, 𝐿], with weight 𝜌, and a high component 𝐻 1 on [𝑐 − , 𝑐 + ]. Either component may be atomless or
have atoms. Proposition A.4 keeps parts (i) and (ii) of Proposition 2 under (H2), 𝐿 < 𝐵1 (𝑚) and
𝐵0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 − < 𝑐 + < 𝐵1 (𝑀). The low component then prepares at every feasible belief, so entry
stays at least 𝜌, and the trading bounds of Lemma 3 apply unchanged. Every entry comparison is
strict, so the outcome is the same under every pair of tie rules.
    In the benchmark, take 𝐿 = 1/5 and a high component uniform on [𝑐 𝐻 − 𝜀, 𝑐 𝐻 + 𝜀]. With
𝜀 = 1/10, entry at 𝑟 1 is 0.522757, the value of the two-point law. The return to the floor needs
a narrower band. At 𝑟 2 , gross profit at the top posterior lies 0.002689 below 𝑐 𝐻 , inside this
band, and entry at 𝑟 2 is 0.382711. Every half-width below 0.002688902 keeps all three parts of
Proposition 2. An atomless high component turns the drop at the ceiling into a ramp: full-order
entry 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜋𝜆) 𝐻 1 𝐵𝑟 (𝑀) is continuous and nonincreasing in strength.
                                (︁     )︁



6.2    Value classes and priors
The reversal holds for every pair of ranked classes and every prior. Take classes that satisfy
Assumption 1 with different means, and any reserve, prior, zero-cost probability and slot. For
uniform incumbents, a nonempty open set of strengths and costs then satisfies (H1)–(H3) and the
ownership condition of Proposition 2 (Proposition A.5).
     The construction puts the weak and the strong incumbent just above the lowest value 𝑣 ∗ at which
the classes start to differ above the reserve. The spread vanishes near 𝑣 ∗ , so informed trade against
the weaker incumbent cannot cover its cost. The profit gap stays near the full class gap there, so
a high cost that only a favorable price justifies fits inside it. In the point-mass case 𝑣 ∗ = ℓ, so the
reversal holds for every ℎ > ℓ.
     Table A.4 in Appendix A.6 reports six economies with their own costs and strengths (computer-
assisted). A prior away from one half needs its own costs. With the benchmark costs and strengths, a
prior of 3/10 leaves entry at 0.250000 at all three strengths. A prior of 7/10 recruits every challenger
at 𝑟 0 already.
     Dispersion within a class also matters. With wide uniform classes, entry rises from 0.250000
to 0.573175, and high-class ownership rises although the high class can lose to the strong
incumbent. Point masses at the same means leave entry at 0.250000. A prepared challenger
keeps (𝜃 − max{𝑝, 𝑅})+ , a convex function of its value, so a mean-preserving spread within a class
raises gross profit at every belief.
     With more than two classes, the result holds under a separation condition (Proposition A.6).
The price reveals only the posterior mean of class proceeds. Separation asks for a gap between


                                                  31
the proceeds of each high-group class and every feasible posterior mean, and the same gap for the
low group. In the strong equilibrium, class proportions within each group then stay at their prior
proportions, so the price reveals the whole posterior vector.
    Without separation, a strict three-class equilibrium has two orders with different posterior vectors
and one price (computer-assisted). This collision is a knife-edge in the class laws. A four-class
economy near the benchmark satisfies separation with distance 0.178871.


6.3    Complementary private signals
The challenger can gain from the price even when its own information is more accurate than the
investor’s. A buyer may know its integration plans, while investors who follow the target know its
customers or its product market. Proposition A.7 represents the two sources with conditionally
independent binary signals. The investor’s signal has accuracy 𝑎𝑇 , and the challenger’s signal has
accuracy 𝑑.
    The zero-cost challenger still prepares after every price and signal, so the price still reveals the
public posterior. The challenger combines that posterior with its own signal. Under strict conditions
on the costs, the weak incumbent has zero orders and entry 𝜌. The strong incumbent has full orders
and entry above 𝜌. The conditions allow 𝑑 > 𝑎𝑇 .
    The example uses its own parameter vector 𝑆(𝐵). It keeps the slot, the values, the reserve and
the prior of 𝐵, and it sets 𝜌 = 0.85, 𝑐 𝐻 = 7.14, 𝑘 = 0.015, 𝑟 0 = 1.1 and 𝑟 1 = 2.3. With investor
accuracy 𝑎𝑇 = 0.70 and challenger accuracy 𝑑 = 0.75, entry rises from 0.850000 to 0.879797
(computer-assisted). A favorable signal alone does not justify high-cost preparation against the weak
incumbent. A favorable signal and a favorable price together do so against the strong incumbent.
Both sources are needed: without the challenger’s signal, entry is 0.850000 at both strengths.
    At the benchmark 𝐵, the conditions cannot hold with 𝑑 ≥ 𝑎𝑇 : the strong trading bound needs an
accurate investor signal, and then the weak bound fails. They hold at 𝐵 with (𝑎𝑇 , 𝑑) = (0.94, 0.505).
Entry then rises from 0.250000 to 0.386933, and without the challenger’s signal it is 0.250000 at
both strengths (computer-assisted). With the accuracies of 𝑆(𝐵), the benchmark 𝐵 has a unique
equilibrium at each strength under rule IM. In it, both classes mix between trade and abstention, and
entry falls from 0.625000 to 0.512521. Under rule I0, the two economies have no equilibrium.


6.4    Order sizes
Remark 3 (Continuous order sizes). The unit menu {−1, 0, 1} keeps every order on path, so no
off-path belief enters the analysis. A menu of continuous order sizes needs a convention for the
sizes that no class uses in equilibrium, as in Edmans et al. (2015). Another route keeps every size
on path through additive liquidity flow. Take Laplace noise of scale 𝑠 and full unit orders. The

                                                  32
largest likelihood ratio of a favorable flow is e2/𝑠 , so the top posterior equals the slot’s 𝑀 (𝜋) when
1 + 𝑢 = e2/𝑠 . The two set-ups then share the posterior bounds. They differ in the probability with
which each class reaches the top posterior.


7     Welfare and sale terms

7.1    Access to prices
Access to prices improves acquisition outcomes at a fixed level of competition. Hold the incumbent
at 𝐹1 . Compare the equilibrium of Proposition 2 with that of an economy in which the challenger
does not see the price. Under the hypotheses of Proposition 2, both economies have unique full
orders. Price access raises expected target proceeds and acquisition surplus net of preparation costs
(Proposition A.8). Acquisition surplus excludes transfers between bidders, shareholders and traders.
    The two economies generate the same orders. The trading bound of Lemma 3 forces full orders
even when the challenger cannot use the price and entry stays at 𝜌. Trading costs therefore coincide.
Price access changes only which high-cost challengers prepare: it adds preparation after a purchase.
Each added preparation contributes its gross profit minus its cost, and also the sales that the reserve
would otherwise block. A challenger prepares only when its expected gross profit covers its cost, so
each term is positive.
    In the benchmark at 𝑟 1 , expected proceeds rise from 0.614583 to 0.872531, a gain of 0.257948.
Net surplus rises by 0.081960. With research at the cap (Section 5), the gains are the same.
    Target shareholders gain because a shareholder who holds through the sale receives 𝑉𝑇 , not
the interim price. The informed investor’s expected gross profit, 0.037665 at 𝑟 1 , is a transfer from
liquidity traders. Suppose that one order unit is 𝑧 shares per share of float. Relative to an economy
without the informed investor, initial shareholders gain in total at every 𝑧 < 6.848394, even if they
bear the whole transfer.
    The gain needs the recruitment region of Proposition 2, in which high-cost preparation does not
pay at the prior. When it already pays at the prior, an informative price deters some preparation.
Keep the strong benchmark and set 𝑐 𝐻 = 12/5: price access then changes proceeds by −0.173917
and net surplus by −0.013505 (Appendix A.7). The information in the price, not its level, separates
the two economies. A deterministic external dividend of 0.257948 in the hidden-price economy
equalizes the mean prices, and it leaves the orders and entry unchanged.




                                                  33
7.2    The payment rule
The effect of competition on the target-payoff spread depends on the sale payment. Proposition A.9
isolates this dependence in a verifiable-value institution with zero reserve. After diligence, a sale to
the next buyer at its value is an enforceable fallback. The seller and the winner divide the surplus
above the fallback by Nash bargaining, with seller weight 𝜔 ∈ [0, 1). A stronger incumbent weakly
lowers the challenger’s profit. Its effect on the spread weakly has the sign of 1 − 2𝜔, and strictly
when E[(𝑅 − ℓ)+ ] changes strictly.
    To see why, take 𝑅 > ℓ. A high-class challenger wins and pays (1 − 𝜔)𝑅 + 𝜔ℎ, whose sensitivity
to 𝑅 is 1 − 𝜔. A low-class challenger loses, and the incumbent pays (1 − 𝜔)ℓ + 𝜔𝑅, whose sensitivity
is 𝜔. Competition widens the difference exactly when the first sensitivity exceeds the second.
    In the benchmark, the spread under this rule changes by 13/20 (1 − 2𝜔) from 𝑟 0 to 𝑟 1 . The
second-price auction is the case 𝜔 = 0: it puts the whole weight on the runner-up’s value. Bulow
and Klemperer (1996) compare an auction with one more bidder and an optimal negotiation by
expected revenue; here the comparison concerns how competition moves the spread. The trading
and preparation equilibrium under the bargaining rule is open.


7.3    Reserves
A reserve affects both the payment required to win and the information in the target’s shares. Once
the reserve can exclude bidders, three events separate. Preparation is the event that the challenger
pays its preparation cost. A sale occurs when at least one bidder submits an admissible bid. Two
admissible bidders meet when both the incumbent and a prepared challenger meet the reserve. In
the benchmark of Section 4.2, the reserve lies below both challenger values, so every prepared
challenger is admissible.
    A reserve that excludes the low class can widen the class difference in target proceeds, because
the low class then adds nothing to proceeds. Informed trade can then pay against both incumbents,
and the price is informative at both strengths. Proposition A.10 gives the consequence. If the top
posterior recruits at both strengths, entry is flat in incumbent strength. Strict deterrence needs the
stronger incumbent to push gross profit at the top posterior below the high cost.
    Table 5 reports three reserves in the benchmark (computer-assisted). The reserve 101/100
excludes the low class. Full orders are unique at both strengths, only a purchase recruits, and entry
is 0.522757 at both 𝑟 0 and 𝑟 1 . This reserve raises expected proceeds at both strengths. Narrow
uniform classes give the same pattern at the reserve 11/10.
    The reserve 31/20 restores strict deterrence: entry is 0.522757 at 𝑟 0 and 0.250000 at 𝑟 1 , and
proceeds exceed their levels at the reserve 1/2. This reserve excludes the low class and also the
weak incumbent, since it exceeds 𝑟 0 . In the benchmark, strict deterrence after the low class is


                                                  34
                           Table 5. Reserves, preparation and sale in the benchmark.
