excluded requires this second exclusion. With a reserve in (ℓ, 𝑟 0 ], the top posterior recruits at
both strengths, and entry is flat. Strict deterrence holds for reserves between 𝑝 𝐻 ≈ 1.325270 and
ℎ − 𝑐 𝐻 /𝑀 ≈ 1.792723 (Appendix A.7). The seller’s choice of a reserve with the whole continuation
retained is open.


8    Empirical implications
The model gives predictions for publicly visible sale processes in which one bidder is prepared
and a further buyer has not yet committed to diligence. This section states them for the benchmark
auction at the prior 1/2, with a slot in the band of Section 4.2. Appendix A.8 states each result in
full and proves it. Full two-sided trade is the unique outcome at every strength 𝑟 with 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 < ℎ,
where 𝑟 𝑆 solves 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝑚 = 𝑘 exactly. This interval contains 𝑟 1 , 𝑟𝐶 and 𝑟 2 . Under full two-sided
trade, the informed share of nonzero orders is 𝜗 = 𝑢/(2 + 𝑢).

Entry and incumbent strength. Proposition 2 and Proposition A.3 compare processes with
prepared bidders of different public strength. When the price reaches the further buyer before
its decision, a stronger prepared bidder can raise the probability that the buyer prepares. In the
benchmark, it rises by 0.272757 from 𝑟 0 to 𝑟 1 . When the price does not reach the buyer, a stronger
prepared bidder does not raise it. Against a strong enough prepared bidder, preparation returns to its
floor while the price stays informative.

The spread and its entry component. The spread is the difference between the conditional
execution prices of a unit purchase and a unit sale. It has a class component 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗 and an entry
component, which is positive exactly when a purchase recruits (Proposition A.11). The sale impact
contains only half of the class component. The purchase impact therefore exceeds the sale impact


                                                            35
exactly when a purchase recruits the buyer, and this test needs no knowledge of the auction objects.
    In the benchmark at 𝑟 1 , the spread is 0.786299, and its entry component is 90.2% of it (computer-
assisted). With a hidden price, the spread would be 0.077020. At 𝑟 2 the spread is 0.108469, the
class component alone, and at 𝑟𝐶 it falls by 0.861835.

The informed share. Order frequencies alone leave the slot open. Under full two-sided trade, the
order law depends on (𝜆, 𝛽) only through the volume of nonzero orders (Proposition A.12). The
investor’s incentives limit the shares that the counts allow. At 𝑟 1 , they allow every informed share
from ≈ 0.250299 up to, but not including, 0.760000.
     A slot with share 0.444444 has the benchmark volume and the benchmark entry, but its spread
at 𝑟 1 is 0.778935, against 0.786299. Prices therefore separate slots that counts cannot. Given the
entry floor and the target-payoff spread, the sale impact and the volume identify the informed share.

Winners. Among prepared challengers, the share of the high class at each price is the price-implied
posterior, which rises with the price (Proposition A.13). A prepared high-class challenger always
outbids the incumbent, and a low-class one loses whenever the incumbent’s value exceeds ℓ. Orders
therefore predict the winner even where they no longer predict entry. In the benchmark, a prepared
challenger wins more often after a purchase than after a sale, by 0.308078 at 𝑟 1 and by 0.333751 at
𝑟 2 . The covariance of the order with entry is 0.272757 at 𝑟 1 and zero at 𝑟 2 , while its covariance with
a challenger win stays positive.

Announcements. Add a public event after the price that reveals only whether the challenger
prepared. The mean price change over both reports, with their model probabilities, is zero. The
mean change in a sample of preparation reports alone is positive (Proposition A.14): 0.160592 at 𝑟 1
in the benchmark. Below the ceiling, an order opportunity with no order is bad news about entry
and no news about the class. At 𝑟 1 , the price after no order minus the expected price before the
opportunity is −0.257948.

Two clusters at intermediate strengths. On (𝑟 𝐹 , 𝑟 𝑁 ), Section 4.3 gives two equilibria under rule
C1. In one, there is no informed trade, the mean order is zero and the three prices coincide. In the
other, the high class buys and the low class abstains. The mean order is then 𝜆/2 (0.168061 in the
benchmark), and every challenger prepares after a purchase. A sale executes above the price after
no order, by 0.005363 at 1.60.
    Under rule C1, processes at one public strength in this range therefore form two clusters. Under
rule CM, the threshold equilibrium of Remark 1 adds a third pattern. Which equilibrium a process
selects is open.


                                                    36
Measurement. The counterpart of preparation is the start of substantive diligence or a proposal
that requires costly preparation; public offers are a narrower outcome. Incumbent strength is
measured from information that is public before that decision, and the price must precede it.
    The order is the first prespecified public order opportunity after the sale becomes public, and an
opportunity with no trade is recorded as a zero. A sample of first executed trades omits the zero
order, so it cannot identify the volume, the effect of an inactive opportunity, or the informed share.
The announcement formulas need events that reveal only preparation. Target returns before a bid
also partly reflect anticipation of that bid (Schwert, 1996), so each test dates the price before the
buyer’s decision. The trading counterpart is outside trade with a view on the further buyer’s fit, such
as flow from sector specialists.


9    Conclusion
The bidder pool in a takeover depends on the information that buyers have before they commit to
preparation. A stronger incumbent moves the class value gap from the challenger’s profit to target
proceeds. In the order slot, a liquidity order in the same direction is always possible. An investor
who knows the challenger’s class therefore keeps an advantage after every order. A larger spread
can make that advantage worth the cost of trade. The price then recruits a challenger that a weaker
incumbent could not attract.
    The comparison holds with unique outcomes on an open set of primitives, and the admissible
region is exact at the benchmark. Between the two economies, informative and uninformative
equilibria coexist. Along the informative branch, entry rises weakly with incumbent strength, and
strictly where the investor mixes. When information is costly, competition creates research: research
and entry rise together along a branch beside the equilibrium without research.
    Sale terms affect participation through the buyer’s expected payment and through the information
in the stock price. In the recruitment region, access to prices raises target proceeds and acquisition
surplus net of preparation costs. The weight that the payment rule places on the runner-up’s value
decides how competition moves the spread. A reserve that excludes the low class makes the price
informative against both incumbents, and entry is then flat in incumbent strength.
    The next theoretical step is the seller’s choice of terms with the whole pricing and trading
continuation retained. The empirical test is whether further preparation in visible sale processes
responds to incumbent strength when the price precedes the decision. A second test is whether
purchase impacts carry the entry component that Section 8 predicts.

A     Proofs
This appendix proves every result in the paper. Each statement carries its status once: analytical,
computer-assisted, numerical diagnostic, or open. Statements use the class model of Section 2.
A statement that needs the point-mass benchmark says so. Each equilibrium proof states the
equilibrium tuple of Definition 1, lists its conditions, and checks each condition against the fixed
tuple.


A.1    Acquisition payoffs
Truthful bids. Fix a bidder with value 𝑣, and let 𝑌 ≥ 0 be the highest competing admissible
bid, with 𝑌 = 0 when there is none. Any admissible bid that wins pays max{𝑝, 𝑌 }, so a bid yields
either 𝑣 − max{𝑝, 𝑌 } or zero. Suppose 𝑣 ≥ 𝑝, and consider the bid 𝑣. If 𝑣 > max{𝑝, 𝑌 }, it wins
and earns 𝑣 − max{𝑝, 𝑌 } > 0. If 𝑣 < max{𝑝, 𝑌 }, then 𝑌 > 𝑣, and the bid loses and earns zero. If
𝑣 = max{𝑝, 𝑌 }, a win pays 𝑣, so the bid earns zero.
    The bid 𝑣 therefore earns (𝑣 − max{𝑝, 𝑌 })+ , the larger of the two possible payoffs, at every 𝑌 .
Suppose 𝑣 < 𝑝. Every bid that wins pays at least 𝑝 > 𝑣, so no bid earns more than zero, the payoff
of no bid. Truthful bids, and no bid below the reserve, are therefore weakly dominant at every
competing bid. This verifies condition (E5) of Definition 1 for every tuple. No payoff below rests on
a belief.

Proof of Proposition 1. Without entry the target receives 𝑝1{𝑅 ≥ 𝑝}. A prepared challenger with
𝜃 ≥ 𝑝 wins at 𝜃 > 𝑅 and pays max{𝑝, 𝑅}. A loss makes the incumbent pay 𝜃. The target receives
max{𝑝, min(𝑅, 𝜃)}, and the challenger keeps (𝜃 − max{𝑝, 𝑅})+ . At 𝜃 < 𝑝 the challenger does not
bid. The target again receives 𝑝1{𝑅 ≥ 𝑝}, and the challenger keeps nothing.
    The benchmark. Let 𝑅 be uniform on [0, 𝑟] with 0 < 𝑝 < ℓ < 𝑟 < ℎ, as in (1). Then
𝑡0 = Pr(𝑅 ≥ 𝑝) 𝑝 = 𝑝(1 − 𝑝/𝑟). Since 𝑅 ≤ 𝑟 < ℎ, the high-value challenger always wins and pays
max{𝑝, 𝑅}. The low-value challenger wins and pays max{𝑝, 𝑅} when 𝑅 < ℓ; it loses when 𝑅 ≥ ℓ.

Proposition 1(c) (the weakest restriction; analytical). Let 𝐺 𝐻 and 𝐺 𝐿 have finite means. The
spread weakly increases under every first-order stochastic strengthening of every continuous 𝐹
if and only if 𝐷 (𝑦) ≥ 0 for every 𝑦 > 𝑝 and 𝐷 ( 𝑝 − ) ≤ 0. When neither class has an atom at 𝑝,
this condition is exactly first-order dominance above the reserve together with equal admissibility,
𝐺 𝐻 ( 𝑝 − ) = 𝐺 𝐿 ( 𝑝 − ).

Proof of Proposition 1(c). Fix the incumbent’s value 𝑅. Suppose 𝑅 ≥ 𝑝. The target receives
min{𝑅, max{𝜃, 𝑝}} for every challenger value. An admissible challenger and the incumbent both

target receives 𝑝1{𝜃 ≥ 𝑝}.

    For 𝑅 ≥ 𝑝, E 𝑗 [min{𝑅, max{𝜃, 𝑝}}] = 𝑝 + 𝑝 [1 − 𝐺 𝑗 (𝑦)] 𝑑𝑦. The expected difference in

    The function 𝜉 is bounded, since 𝑝 |𝐷 (𝑦)| 𝑑𝑦 ≤ E𝐻 𝜃 + E 𝐿 𝜃 < ∞. It is constant below 𝑝,
continuous on [ 𝑝, ∞), and has derivative 𝐷 almost everywhere above 𝑝. It moves from 𝑝𝐷 ( 𝑝 − )
to 𝜉 ( 𝑝) = 0 at 𝑝. Thus 𝜉 is nondecreasing if and only if 𝐷 ≥ 0 almost everywhere above 𝑝 and
𝐷 ( 𝑝 − ) ≤ 0.
    If 𝜉 is nondecreasing, every first-order stochastic strengthening raises E𝐹 [𝜉 (𝑅)]. If it is not,
continuity on each side of 𝑝 gives values 0 < 𝑅1 < 𝑅2 , both different from 𝑝, with 𝜉 (𝑅1 ) > 𝜉 (𝑅2 ).
Let 𝐹 be uniform on [𝑅1 − 𝛿, 𝑅1 + 𝛿], and let 𝐹˜ be the same law shifted to 𝑅2 . Then 𝐹˜ ≤ 𝐹, and as

    Suppose the support must start at zero. Mix the same small uniform component on [0, 𝑟]    ¯ into
both laws. Both mixtures are continuous, and the mixture scales the difference by a positive factor
and keeps its sign.
    Distribution functions are right-continuous. Thus 𝐷 ≥ 0 almost everywhere above 𝑝 is the
same as 𝐷 (𝑦) ≥ 0 for every 𝑦 > 𝑝, and it implies 𝐷 ( 𝑝) ≥ 0. When neither class has an atom at 𝑝,
𝐷 ( 𝑝 − ) = 𝐷 ( 𝑝) ≥ 0. The condition then holds exactly when 𝐷 ( 𝑝 − ) = 0, which with the ranking
above the reserve is equal admissibility. □

Scope of Assumption 1. The proofs in this subsection use Assumption 1 only through 𝐷 (𝑦) ≥ 0

A.2    Beliefs and trading
This subsection proves the results of Sections 3.2 and 3.3. Each result holds for every order profile,
in or out of equilibrium. Every later proof uses them in that form.

Conventions for Appendices A.2 to A.9.        The conditions (E1) to (E6) are those of Definition 1.

   • A profile is a pair (𝜎𝐻 , 𝜎𝐿 ) of order distributions, pure or mixed, with wrong-signed orders
     allowed. A candidate is a tuple E that satisfies (E1) to (E4) and (E6) for some profile. An
     equilibrium is a candidate that also satisfies (E5).



                                                 44
   • A deviation by the investor holds the candidate’s price function 𝑃 and preparation rule 𝜁 fixed.
     It changes only the order that reaches them.

   • A uniqueness proof derives its bounds for every candidate. It does not use the proposed tuple.

   • A verification checks each condition by direct computation against the stated tuple. It does not
     cite a lemma whose premises include the condition under check.

   • Write 𝑤 𝑗 = 𝑡 𝑗 − 𝑡 0 for the gain in expected proceeds that entry brings in class 𝑗, and
     𝑤(𝜇) = (1 − 𝜇)𝑤 𝐿 + 𝜇𝑤 𝐻 = 𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡 0 + Δ𝑇 𝜇 for its average at the belief 𝜇. Proposition 1 and
     Section 3.1 give 𝑤 𝐻 ≥ 𝑤 𝐿 ≥ 0.

   • A computer-assisted step names its script and its run log. All script paths start at the folder
     audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/ of the project repository.

Proof of Lemma 1. Fix any profile (𝜎𝐻 , 𝜎𝐿 ). By (8), the posterior 𝜇(𝑥) increases strictly in the
likelihood ratio 𝑓𝐻 (𝑥)/ 𝑓 𝐿 (𝑥). The odds of 𝑀 (𝜋) are 𝜋(1 + 𝑢)/(1 − 𝜋), and the odds of 𝑚(𝜋) are
𝜋/[(1 − 𝜋) (1 + 𝑢)].
    Part (a). The liquidity trader fills the slot with probability 1 − 𝜆 > 0. It places the order 𝑥 with
probability 𝜂(𝑥), and 𝜂(𝑥) > 0 for every 𝑥 because 0 < 𝛽 < 1/2. The informed term 𝜆𝜎 𝑗 (𝑥) is
nonnegative. Hence 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) ≥ (1 − 𝜆)𝜂(𝑥) > 0 for every order and class. Every order has positive
probability in both classes, so (E1) defines 𝜇(𝑥) at every order, and no off-path belief enters any
condition. Also 0 < 𝜇(𝑥) < 1, since 0 < 𝜋 < 1.
    Part (b). For 𝑥 = ±1, (1 − 𝜆)𝜂(𝑥) = 𝑏 and 0 ≤ 𝜆𝜎 𝑗 (𝑥) ≤ 𝜆. Hence 𝑏 ≤ 𝑓 𝑗 (𝑥) ≤ 𝑏 + 𝜆, and

                              1   𝑏    𝑓𝐻 (𝑥) 𝑏 + 𝜆
                                =    ≤         ≤    = 1 + 𝑢.
                             1+𝑢 𝑏+𝜆   𝑓 𝐿 (𝑥)   𝑏

By (8), 𝑚(𝜋) ≤ 𝜇(𝑥) ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋). This step uses only 0 < 𝛽 < 1/2.
    Part (c). For 𝑥 = 0, (1 − 𝜆)𝜂(0) = 𝑏 0 , so 𝑏 0 ≤ 𝑓 𝑗 (0) ≤ 𝑏 0 + 𝜆, and the likelihood ratio
lies in [(1 + 𝜆/𝑏 0 ) −1 , 1 + 𝜆/𝑏 0 ]. The inequality 𝑏 0 ≥ 𝑏 is 1 − 2𝛽 ≥ 𝛽, that is 𝛽 ≤ 1/3. Then
𝜆/𝑏 0 ≤ 𝜆/𝑏 = 𝑢, and the argument of part (b) gives 𝑚(𝜋) ≤ 𝜇(0) ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋). If 𝛽 > 1/3, then
𝑏 0 < 𝑏. The profile 𝜎𝐻 = 𝛿0 with 𝜎𝐿 (0) = 0 gives 𝑓𝐻 (0) = 𝑏 0 + 𝜆 and 𝑓 𝐿 (0) = 𝑏 0 . The ratio is
1 + 𝜆/𝑏 0 > 1 + 𝑢, so 𝜇(0) > 𝑀 (𝜋), and the bound fails.
    Part (d). The profile 𝜎𝐻 = 𝛿1 , 𝜎𝐿 = 𝛿−1 gives 𝑓𝐻 = (𝑏, 𝑏 0 , 𝑏 + 𝜆) and 𝑓 𝐿 = (𝑏 + 𝜆, 𝑏 0 , 𝑏) on
the orders (−1, 0, 1). The likelihood ratios are (1 + 𝑢) −1 , 1 and 1 + 𝑢, so (𝜇(−1), 𝜇(0), 𝜇(1)) =
(𝑚(𝜋), 𝜋, 𝑀 (𝜋)).
    Beliefs at a price. Fix a price 𝑃¯ in the image of 𝑃, and let 𝑋 ( 𝑃)

Proof of Lemma 2. Fix a candidate, and let Δ𝑇 > 0 and 𝑔𝐻 > 𝑔 𝐿 . The proof has a preliminary step
and five parts.
    Step 0: the terminal payoff given the class and the order. The challenger’s choice depends only
on the price 𝑃(𝑥) and its cost 𝐶. The cost is independent of the class, the values, the incumbent’s
value and the slot draws. Given the class 𝑗 and the order 𝑥, the challenger therefore prepares with
probability 𝑒(𝑥) = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)𝜁 (𝑃(𝑥)), and this event is independent of (𝜃, 𝑅). The order depends
on the class and the slot draws only, so it carries no information about (𝜃, 𝑅) given 𝑗. Bids are
truthful by (E6). With entry, expected proceeds in class 𝑗 are 𝑡 𝑗 ; without entry, they are 𝑡0 . Hence

                                  E[𝑉𝑇 | 𝑗, 𝑋 = 𝑥] = 𝑡0 + 𝑒(𝑥) 𝑤 𝑗 .                             (A.3)

   Part (a): the floor. The zero-cost challenger prepares after every price (Definition 1), and it has
probability 𝜌. Hence 𝑒(𝑥) ≥ 𝜌 at every order.
   Part (b): the price. Average (A.3) over the class with the weights 𝜇(𝑥) and 1 − 𝜇(𝑥) of (E1).
Condition (E4) gives 𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑡0 + 𝑒(𝑥) 𝑤(𝜇(𝑥)), which is (4). This step uses only (E1), (E4) and the
independence of the cost.
   Part (c): sufficiency. Let 𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑃(𝑥 ′). Entry depends on the order only through the price,
so 𝑒(𝑥) = 𝑒(𝑥 ′) =: 𝑒,¯ and 𝑒¯ ≥ 𝜌 > 0 by part (a). Part (b) gives 𝑒¯ Δ𝑇 [𝜇(𝑥) − 𝜇(𝑥 ′)] = 0, so
𝜇(𝑥) = 𝜇(𝑥 ′). By the step on beliefs at a price in the proof of Lemma 1, 𝜇(𝑃(𝑥))
                                                                            ¯        lies between the
smallest and the largest posterior of the orders with the price 𝑃(𝑥). These posteriors are equal, so
 ¯
𝜇(𝑃(𝑥))  = 𝜇(𝑥). The price therefore reveals the posterior.


                                                  46
   Part (d): entry. Since 𝑔𝐻 > 𝑔 𝐿 , 𝐵(𝜇) > 𝑐 𝐻 exactly when 𝜇 > 𝜏. By part (c), the challenger’s
gross profit at 𝑃(𝑥) is 𝐵(𝜇(𝑥)). Condition (E3) gives 𝑒(𝑥) = 𝜌 if 𝜇(𝑥) < 𝜏 and 𝑒(𝑥) = 1 if 𝜇(𝑥) > 𝜏.
At 𝜇(𝑥) = 𝜏, 𝑒(𝑥) = 1 under rule C1, and 𝑒(𝑥) can take any value in [𝜌, 1] under rule CM.
   Part (e): monotonicity. Let 𝜇(𝑥) > 𝜇(𝑥 ′). If 𝜇(𝑥 ′) < 𝜏, then 𝑒(𝑥 ′) = 𝜌 ≤ 𝑒(𝑥). If 𝜇(𝑥 ′) ≥ 𝜏,
then 𝜇(𝑥) > 𝜏 and 𝑒(𝑥) = 1 ≥ 𝑒(𝑥 ′). Part (b) gives

                𝑃(𝑥) − 𝑃(𝑥 ′) = 𝑒(𝑥) Δ𝑇 [𝜇(𝑥) − 𝜇(𝑥 ′)] + [𝑒(𝑥) − 𝑒(𝑥 ′)] 𝑤(𝜇(𝑥 ′)).               (A.4)

The second term is nonnegative, because 𝑤(𝜇) ≥ 𝑤 𝐿 ≥ 0. The first term is at least 𝜌Δ𝑇 [𝜇(𝑥) −
𝜇(𝑥 ′)] > 0. Prices therefore rise strictly with the posterior.                              □

Constructed tuples. The identity (A.4) uses only the price formula. It therefore applies to

nondecreasing in the posterior. The verifications below use it in this form, before any condition is
checked: a stated tuple has distinct prices at distinct posteriors, and equal prices at equal posteriors.

Proof of Lemma 3. Fix a tuple that satisfies (E1) and (E4), with 𝑒(𝑥) ∈ [𝜌, 1] after every order, and
let Δ𝑇 ≥ 0. A deviation to the order 𝑞 reaches the tuple’s price 𝑃(𝑞) and entry 𝑒(𝑞). By (A.3) and
part (b) of the proof of Lemma 2,

             E[𝑉𝑇 | 𝐻, 𝑋 = 𝑞] − 𝑃(𝑞) = 𝑒(𝑞) [𝑤 𝐻 − 𝑤(𝜇(𝑞))] = 𝑒(𝑞) Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(𝑞)],
             𝑃(𝑞) − E[𝑉𝑇 | 𝐿, 𝑋 = 𝑞] = 𝑒(𝑞) [𝑤(𝜇(𝑞)) − 𝑤 𝐿 ] = 𝑒(𝑞) Δ𝑇 𝜇(𝑞).

The proceeds 𝑡 0 and the term 𝑒(𝑞)𝑤 𝐿 cancel. Multiply by 𝑞 and subtract 𝑘 |𝑞|. This gives the six
payoffs of the lemma, with 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(1)] and 𝐾 𝐿 = 𝑒(−1)Δ𝑇 𝜇(−1).
     Part (a). Since 𝑒 ≥ 0, Δ𝑇 ≥ 0 and 0 ≤ 𝜇 ≤ 1, the payoffs 𝑈𝐻 (−1) = −𝑒(−1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(−1)] − 𝑘
and 𝑈 𝐿 (1) = −𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 𝜇(1) − 𝑘 are at most −𝑘 < 0 = 𝑈 𝑗 (0). No class puts weight on a wrong-signed
order under (E5).
     Part (b). By part (a), class 𝐻 compares the purchase with abstention, and 𝑈𝐻 (1)−𝑈𝐻 (0) = 𝐾 𝐻 −𝑘.
Class 𝐻 buys with probability one if 𝐾 𝐻 > 𝑘 and abstains if 𝐾 𝐻 < 𝑘. If 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑘, the purchase
and abstention are its two maximizers: rule IM allows any mixture of them, and rule I0 selects
abstention. Class 𝐿 compares the sale with abstention, and 𝑈 𝐿 (−1) − 𝑈 𝐿 (0) = 𝐾 𝐿 − 𝑘. Class 𝐿
sells with probability one if 𝐾 𝐿 > 𝑘 and abstains if 𝐾 𝐿 < 𝑘. If 𝐾 𝐿 = 𝑘, the sale and abstention are
its two maximizers: rule IM allows any mixture of them, and rule I0 selects abstention.
     Part (c). By Lemma 1(b), 𝜇(1) ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋) and 𝜇(−1) ≥ 𝑚(𝜋) under every profile, for every




                                                  47
𝛽 < 1/2. Also 𝜌 ≤ 𝑒 ≤ 1 and 0 ≤ 𝜇 ≤ 1. Hence

      𝜌 𝜅(𝜋) Δ𝑇 ≤ 𝜌 [1 − 𝑀 (𝜋)] Δ𝑇 ≤ 𝐾 𝐻 ≤ Δ𝑇 ,                𝜌 𝜅(𝜋) Δ𝑇 ≤ 𝜌 𝑚(𝜋) Δ𝑇 ≤ 𝐾 𝐿 ≤ Δ𝑇 .              (A.5)

The upper bounds use only 𝑒 ≤ 1 and 0 ≤ 𝜇 ≤ 1.
    Part (d). The return 𝐾 𝐻 depends on the tuple only through (𝜇(1), 𝑒(1)), and 𝐾 𝐿 only through
(𝜇(−1), 𝑒(−1)). Entry after the zero order enters no payoff. The ex ante payoff of the investor is
𝜆 𝑈 𝑗 (𝑞), and the factor 𝜆 > 0 changes no comparison.                                          □

Where 𝛽 ≤ 1/3 enters. The bound 𝛽 ≤ 1/3 enters only at the zero order, and through it only
Corollary 1 and the bound 𝜇¯ ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋) at a price that pools the zero order. The bounds (A.5) use the
nonzero orders only. The proofs of Propositions 2 and 3 and of Corollary 2 use parts (a), (b) and (d)
of Lemma 1 only, so these results hold for every 𝛽 < 1/2.


A.3      Competition creates competition
This subsection proves Proposition 2 for general classes and priors. It then states and proves
Proposition A.1, the admissible region at the benchmark. Write 𝐹1 for a strengthening of 𝐹0 and 𝐹2
for a strengthening of 𝐹1 . For 𝑖 ∈ {0, 1, 2}, write 𝐵𝑖 = 𝐵 𝐹𝑖 , Δ𝑖 = Δ𝑇 (𝐹𝑖 ), and 𝑡0,𝑖 , 𝑡 𝑗,𝑖 , 𝑔 𝑗,𝑖 , 𝑤 𝑖 (𝜇) for
the objects at 𝐹𝑖 . Also write 𝑀 = 𝑀 (𝜋), 𝑚 = 𝑚(𝜋) and 𝜅 = 𝜅(𝜋). Under Assumption 1 every value
meets the reserve, so 𝑊𝐻 (𝐹) = E𝐻 [𝐹 (𝜃)], the probability that a prepared high-class challenger
outbids the incumbent. Value ties have probability zero because 𝐹 is continuous.

Proof of Proposition 2. The proof has seven steps. Steps 1, 3 and 5 state the equilibrium tuple
of each economy, check every condition against it, and prove uniqueness from bounds on every
candidate.
    Step 0: implied inequalities. Proposition 1(a) gives 𝐵1 (𝜋) ≤ 𝐵0 (𝜋), so (H1) gives 𝐵1 (𝜋) <
𝑐 𝐻 < 𝐵1 (𝑀). Since 𝑀 > 𝜋, 𝐵1 (𝑀) − 𝐵1 (𝜋) = (𝑀 − 𝜋)(𝑔𝐻,1 − 𝑔 𝐿,1 ) > 0. Hence 𝑔𝐻,1 > 𝑔 𝐿,1 , the
threshold 𝜏1 at 𝐹1 is defined, and 𝜋 < 𝜏1 < 𝑀. By (H2), Δ1 > 𝑘/(𝜌𝜅) > 0. Proposition 1(b) gives
Δ2 ≥ Δ1 , so 𝜌𝜅Δ2 > 𝑘. Proposition 1 also gives 𝑔𝐻,2 ≥ 𝑔 𝐿,2 , so 𝐵2 (𝜇) ≤ 𝐵2 (𝑀) < 𝑐 𝐻 for every
𝜇 ≤ 𝑀. The zero-cost challenger prepares after every price, so Lemma 2(a) gives 𝑒 ≥ 𝜌 in every
candidate of each economy.

Each hypothesis is used once.

   • 𝐵0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 gives entry 𝜌 at 𝐹0 . Through Proposition 1(a), it also stops recruitment after the

The open set. For uniform incumbents, (H1) to (H3) are strict inequalities between continuous
functions of (𝑟 0 , 𝑟 1 , 𝑟 2 , 𝑐 𝐻 , 𝑘, 𝜌, 𝜋, 𝜆, 𝛽). They define an open set in the domain 0 < 𝛽 < 1/2. The
model adds the structural bound 𝛽 ≤ 1/3, and in that domain the set is relatively open. Proposition A.1
gives its section through the benchmark. The sufficient condition (1 + 𝜌)𝑊𝐻 (𝐹1 ) ≥ 2𝜌𝑊𝐻 (𝐹0 ) > 0
for higher ownership implies the exact condition of Step 4 whenever 𝑏 + 𝜆 > 1/2, because then
𝑒 𝐻 > (1 + 𝜌)/2. At calibration B, 𝑏 + 𝜆 = 0.531737.

Proposition A.1 (The admissible region; analytical). Take the benchmark auction and cost primitives,
the strengths 𝑟 0 , 𝑟 1 , 𝑟 2 , and a slot with 0 < 𝜆 < 1 and 0 < 𝛽 ≤ 1/3.

(a) At the prior 1/2, (H1)–(H3) hold if and only if

       82/59 < 𝑢 < 2686/1559,         that is,   𝜏(𝑟 1 ) = 141/200 < 𝑀 (1/2) < 4245/5804 = 𝜏(𝑟 2 ).

     Since 𝜆 = 𝑢𝛽/(1 + 𝑢𝛽) rises with 𝑢, the band is an open interval of 𝜆 at each 𝛽.

Proof of Proposition A.1. Take the benchmark primitives (ℎ, ℓ, 𝑝) = (10, 1, 1/2), (𝜌, 𝑐 𝐻 , 𝑘) =
(1/4, 6, 1/50) and the strengths (𝑟 0 , 𝑟 1 , 𝑟 2 ) = (6/5, 3, 18/5). Then 𝜏(𝑟) = (48𝑟−3)/[4(20𝑟−𝑟 2 −1)],
with 𝜏(𝑟 0 ) = 195/308, 𝜏(𝑟 1 ) = 141/200 and 𝜏(𝑟 2 ) = 4245/5804. Also Δ𝑇 (𝑟 0 ) = 1/60, Δ𝑇 (𝑟 1 ) =
2/3 and Δ𝑇 (𝑟 2 ) = 169/180 by (5).
    Part (a). At 𝜋 = 1/2, 𝑀 = (1 + 𝑢)/(2 + 𝑢) and 𝜅 = 𝑚 = 1 − 𝑀 = 1/(2 + 𝑢). The left
side of (H1) has the margin 𝑐 𝐻 − 𝐵𝑟0 (1/2) = 1.195833, and the left side of (H2) has the margin
𝑘 − Δ𝑇 (𝑟 0 ) = 0.003333. Neither depends on the slot. The right side of (H1) is 𝑀 > 𝜏(𝑟 1 ),
that is 200(1 + 𝑢) > 141(2 + 𝑢), that is 𝑢 > 82/59. Hypothesis (H3) is 𝑀 < 𝜏(𝑟 2 ), that is
5804(1 + 𝑢) < 4245(2 + 𝑢), that is 𝑢 < 2686/1559. The right side of (H2) is 𝜌(1 − 𝑀)Δ𝑇 (𝑟 1 ) > 𝑘,
that is (1 − 𝑀)/6 > 1/50, that is 𝑀 < 22/25, that is 𝑢 < 19/3. The bound 𝑢 < 2686/1559
implies it; at the benchmark slot 19/3 − 𝑢 = 4.615052. Hence (H1) to (H3) hold if and only if
82/59 < 𝑢 < 2686/1559, that is 𝜏(𝑟 1 ) < 𝑀 < 𝜏(𝑟 2 ). At fixed 𝛽, 𝜆 = 𝑢𝛽/(1 + 𝑢𝛽) increases strictly
in 𝑢, so the band is an open interval of 𝜆.
    Part (b). For 𝑢 < 19/3, Step 2 of the proof of Proposition 2 holds at 𝑟 1 , and at 𝑟 2 because
Δ𝑇 (𝑟 2 ) > Δ𝑇 (𝑟 1 ). Orders are full at both strengths, and the posteriors are (𝑚, 1/2, 𝑀). If 𝑢 < 82/59,
then 𝑀 < 𝜏(𝑟 1 ), no order recruits at 𝑟 1 , and entry at 𝑟 1 is 𝜌. At 𝑢 = 82/59, a purchase leaves the
challenger indifferent: rule C1 gives entry E1 , and rule CM allows entry 𝜌. If 2686/1559 ≤ 𝑢 < 19/3,
then 𝑀 ≥ 𝜏(𝑟 2 ), and a purchase recruits at 𝑟 2 under rule C1. For 𝑢 ≥ 19/3, the right side of (H2)
fails at 𝑟 1 .
    Part (c). Fix 1 + 𝑢 = e and the other benchmark inputs, and vary 𝜋. The inequality 𝑀 (𝜋) > 𝜏 is
𝜋/(1 − 𝜋) > 𝜏/[(1 − 𝜏)(1 + 𝑢)], that is 𝜋 > 𝜏/[𝜏 + (1 − 𝜏)e]. The right side of (H1) gives the lower
endpoint of the proposition, and (H3) gives its upper endpoint. The left side of (H1) is 𝐵𝑟0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 ,
that is 𝜋 < 𝜏(𝑟 0 ) = 195/308. The left side of (H2) does not depend on 𝜋. The right side of (H2)
is 𝜅(𝜋) > 𝑘/(𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟 1 )) = 3/25: 𝑚(𝜋) > 3/25 is 𝜋 > 3e/(22 + 3e), and 1 − 𝑀 (𝜋) > 3/25 is
𝜋 < 22/(22 + 3e), that is 𝜋 ∈ (0.270432, 0.729568) approximately. This interval contains the other


                                                    52
two. The intersection is the interval of the proposition, with endpoints ≈ 0.467850 and ≈ 0.500424.
Without (H3), the upper endpoint is 𝜏(𝑟 0 ) ≈ 0.633117.                                          □

A.4     Coexistence
This subsection proves Proposition 3 and Corollary 2. Throughout, the auction is the benchmark:
point masses ℎ and ℓ, 𝑅 ∼ 𝑈 [0, 𝑟], and the benchmark values of (ℎ, ℓ, 𝑝, 𝜌, 𝑐 𝐻 , 𝑘). The path is
𝑟 ∈ [𝑟 0 , 𝑟 2 ]. The slot is any pair (𝜆, 𝛽) with 0 < 𝜆 < 1, 0 < 𝛽 < 1/2 and 𝜏(𝑟 1 ) < 𝑀 (1/2) < 𝜏(𝑟 2 ),
the band of Proposition A.1. On the path, 𝑔𝐻 > 𝑔 𝐿 and Δ𝑇 > 0 because ℓ < 𝑟 < ℎ, so Lemma 2
applies at every strength.

posterior 𝜏(𝑟) and recruits,

0 for 𝑟 > ℓ, Δ𝑇 (𝑟) ≤ 𝑑 holds exactly when 𝑟 ≤ 𝔯(𝑑).

Lemma A.1(a)–(b) make 𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝐽 and 𝑟𝐶 unique, and Lemma A.1(d) makes the root 𝑟 ∗ of 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝜏(𝑟) =

Lemma A.2 (The order of the thresholds; computer-assisted). For every slot in the band, 𝑟 0 < 𝑟 𝐴 <
𝑟 𝐹 < 𝑟 𝑁 < 𝑟 𝐽 < 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 1 < 𝑟𝐶 < 𝑟 2 .

Proof. Each inequality reduces to a fact of Lemma A.1 or to the band.

  • 𝑟 0 < 𝑟 𝐴 < 𝑟 1 : 𝐽 (𝑟 0 ) < 𝑘 < 𝜌𝐽 (𝑟 1 ) < 𝐽 (𝑟 1 ), with 𝜌𝐽 (𝑟 1 ) = 59/1200, and 𝐽 is increasing.

  • 𝑟 𝐴 < 𝑟 𝐹 : Δ𝑇 (𝑟 𝐴 ) < 𝑘/(1 − 𝑀) is (1 − 𝑀)Δ𝑇 (𝑟 𝐴 ) < 𝑘 = (1 − 𝜏(𝑟 𝐴 ))Δ𝑇 (𝑟 𝐴 ), that is 𝑀 > 𝜏(𝑟 𝐴 ).
    This holds because 𝑀 > 𝜏(𝑟 1 ) > 𝜏(𝑟 𝐴 ), since 𝑟 𝐴 < 𝑟 1 .

  • 𝑟 𝐹 < 𝑟 𝑁 : 𝑘/(1 − 𝑀) < 2𝑘/𝜌 is 𝑀 < 1 − 𝜌/2. This holds because 𝑀 < 𝜏(𝑟 2 ) < 3/4 < 1 − 𝜌/2.

  • 𝑟 𝑁 < 𝑟 𝐽 : 𝜌𝐽 (𝑟 𝑁 ) = 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟 𝑁 ) [1 − 𝜏(𝑟 𝑁 )] = 2𝑘 [1 − 𝜏(𝑟 𝑁 )] < 𝑘, because 𝜏(𝑟 𝑁 ) > 1/2, with
    𝜏(𝑟 𝑁 ) − 1/2 = 0.154463.

  • 𝑟 𝐽 < 𝑟 𝑆 : Δ𝑇 (𝑟 𝐽 ) < 𝑘/(𝜌𝑚) is 𝜌(1 − 𝑀)Δ𝑇 (𝑟 𝐽 ) < 𝑘 = 𝜌(1 − 𝜏(𝑟 𝐽 ))Δ𝑇 (𝑟 𝐽 ), that is 𝑀 > 𝜏(𝑟 𝐽 ).
    The value 𝜌𝐽 (𝑟 1 ) > 𝑘 gives 𝑟 𝐽 < 𝑟 1 , so 𝑀 > 𝜏(𝑟 1 ) > 𝜏(𝑟 𝐽 ).

  • 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 1 : 𝜌(1 − 𝑀)Δ𝑇 (𝑟 1 ) > 𝑘 is (1 − 𝑀)/6 > 1/50, that is 𝑀 < 22/25. This holds because
    𝑀 < 3/4.

  • 𝑟 1 < 𝑟𝐶 < 𝑟 2 : this is 𝜏(𝑟 1 ) < 𝑀 < 𝜏(𝑟 2 ), the band, since 𝜏 is increasing.                        □

The two investor problems

Fix a strength 𝑟 on the path and a prior 𝜋 < 𝜏(𝑟). By Lemma 3(a), no equilibrium puts weight on
a wrong-signed order. Write 𝑠 = 𝑠+ = 𝜆𝜎𝐻 (1) and 𝑡 = 𝑠− = 𝜆𝜎𝐿 (−1) for the probabilities of an
informed purchase and an informed sale. On the orders (−1, 0, 1),

                    𝑓𝐻 = (𝑏, 𝑏 0 + 𝜆 − 𝑠, 𝑏 + 𝑠),           𝑓 𝐿 = (𝑏 + 𝑡, 𝑏 0 + 𝜆 − 𝑡, 𝑏).

Bayes’ rule gives

                     𝜋(𝑏 + 𝑠)                          (1 − 𝜋)𝑏                                   𝜋𝑏
  𝜇+ (𝑠) := 𝜇(1) =            ,        1 − 𝜇+ (𝑠) =             ,      𝜇− (𝑡) := 𝜇(−1) =                  ,
                      𝑏 + 𝜋𝑠                            𝑏 + 𝜋𝑠                               𝑏 + (1 − 𝜋)𝑡

                                    𝜋
and 𝜇0 (𝑠, 𝑡) := 𝜇(0) has the odds 1−𝜋 · 𝑏𝑏00+𝜆−𝑠
                                             +𝜆−𝑡 . On [0, 𝜆], 𝜇+ is continuous and rises strictly from 𝜋
to 𝑀 (𝜋), and 𝜇− is continuous and falls strictly from 𝜋 to 𝑚(𝜋). When 𝜋 < 𝜏 < 𝑀 (𝜋), the rate

                                                 𝑏(𝜏 − 𝜋)
                                          𝑠∗ =            ∈ (0, 𝜆)
                                                 𝜋(1 − 𝜏)

solves 𝜇+ (𝑠∗ ) = 𝜏. Entry after each order follows from Lemma 2(d).

Lemma A.3 (The high class; analytical). Fix 𝑟 and 𝜋 with 𝜋 < 𝜏(𝑟). In a candidate with no
wrong-signed order, the pair (𝑠, 𝑒(1)) satisfies (E1) to (E3) at the order 1, and (E5) for class 𝐻,
exactly when it is one of these five pairs.

 (N) 𝑠 = 0 and 𝑒(1) = 𝜌, with 𝜌Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝜋) ≤ 𝑘.

 (Q) 0 < 𝑠 < min{𝑠∗ , 𝜆}, 𝑒(1) = 𝜌 and 𝜌Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇+ (𝑠)] = 𝑘. Only under rule IM.

 (R) 𝑠∗ < 𝑠 < 𝜆, 𝑒(1) = 1 and Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇+ (𝑠)] = 𝑘. Only under rule IM.

  (T) 𝑠 = 𝑠∗ ∈ (0, 𝜆) and 𝑒(1) = 𝑒 ∗ := 𝑘/𝐽 (𝑟). Under rule C1 this requires 𝑒 ∗ = 1, that is 𝐽 (𝑟) = 𝑘.
      Under rule CM it requires 𝜌 ≤ 𝑒 ∗ ≤ 1. Only under rule IM.

                                                       56
 (F) 𝑠 = 𝜆. Here 𝑒(1) = 1 if 𝑀 (𝜋) > 𝜏 and 𝑒(1) = 𝜌 if 𝑀 (𝜋) < 𝜏. If 𝑀 (𝜋) = 𝜏, then 𝑒(1) = 1
     under rule C1, and 𝑒(1) ∈ [𝜌, 1] under rule CM. The return must satisfy 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 [1−𝑀 (𝜋)] ≥ 𝑘
     under rule IM, and > 𝑘 under rule I0.

Each equation in (Q) and (R) has at most one solution.

Proof. By Lemma 3(b), (E5) for class 𝐻 requires 𝐾 𝐻 ≤ 𝑘 at 𝑠 = 0. At 𝑠 = 𝜆 it requires 𝐾 𝐻 ≥ 𝑘
under rule IM, and 𝐾 𝐻 > 𝑘 under rule I0, because rule I0 selects abstention at a tie. At 0 < 𝑠 < 𝜆
it requires 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑘. An interior 𝑠 is a mixture at indifference, so it requires rule IM. Here
𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇+ (𝑠)], and 𝑒(1) follows from Lemma 2(d) at the posterior 𝜇+ (𝑠).

   • At 𝑠 = 0, 𝜇+ (0) = 𝜋 < 𝜏, so 𝑒(1) = 𝜌. This is (N).

   • At 0 < 𝑠 < 𝜆 with 𝜇+ (𝑠) < 𝜏: 𝑒(1) = 𝜌, and 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑘 is the equation of (Q).

   • At 0 < 𝑠 < 𝜆 with 𝜇+ (𝑠) > 𝜏: 𝑒(1) = 1, and 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑘 is the equation of (R).

   • At 0 < 𝑠 < 𝜆 with 𝜇+ (𝑠) = 𝜏: then 𝑠 = 𝑠∗ , and 𝐾 𝐻 = 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝜏) = 𝑒(1)𝐽 (𝑟) = 𝑘 gives
     𝑒(1) = 𝑒 ∗ . Rule C1 sets 𝑒(1) = 1. Rule CM allows any 𝑒(1) ∈ [𝜌, 1]. This is (T).

   • At 𝑠 = 𝜆, 𝜇+ (𝜆) = 𝑀 (𝜋). Lemma 2(d) gives 𝑒(1), and the condition on 𝐾 𝐻 is that of (F).

The function 1 − 𝜇+ falls strictly, so each equation has at most one root.                       □

Lemma A.4 (The low class; analytical). Fix 𝑟 and 𝜋 with 𝜋 < 𝜏(𝑟). Then 𝜇− (𝑡) ≤ 𝜋 < 𝜏 for every 𝑡,
so 𝑒(−1) = 𝜌 in every candidate. In a candidate with no wrong-signed order, the rate 𝑡 satisfies
(E1) to (E3) at the order −1, and (E5) for class 𝐿, exactly when it is one of these:

 (N) 𝑡 = 0, with 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋 ≤ 𝑘;

 (Q) 0 < 𝑡 < 𝜆 with 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜇− (𝑡) = 𝑘; only under rule IM;

 (F) 𝑡 = 𝜆, with 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) ≥ 𝑘 under rule IM, and > 𝑘 under rule I0.

Under rule IM exactly one rate qualifies at each 𝑟:

                                   0,                  𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋 ≤ 𝑘,
                                 ⎧
                                 ⎪
                                 ⎪
                                 ⎪
                                 ⎪
                                 ⎨ 𝑏 (𝜋𝜌Δ𝑇 /𝑘 − 1)
                                 ⎪
                                 ⎪
                       𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟) =                   ,   𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) < 𝑘 < 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋,

Under rule I0 no rate qualifies when 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) ≤ 𝑘 < 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋.


                                                  57
Proof. Since 𝜇− is decreasing, 𝜇− (𝑡) ≤ 𝜇− (0) = 𝜋 < 𝜏, so Lemma 2(d) gives 𝑒(−1) = 𝜌. Then
𝐾 𝐿 = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜇− (𝑡) is continuous and strictly decreasing in 𝑡, from 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋 at 𝑡 = 0 to 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) at 𝑡 = 𝜆.
By Lemma 3(b), (E5) for class 𝐿 requires 𝐾 𝐿 ≤ 𝑘 at 𝑡 = 0; 𝐾 𝐿 ≥ 𝑘 at 𝑡 = 𝜆 under rule IM, and
> 𝑘 under rule I0; and 𝐾 𝐿 = 𝑘 at 0 < 𝑡 < 𝜆, under rule IM only. If 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋 ≤ 𝑘, then 𝐾 𝐿 < 𝑘 for
every 𝑡 > 0, so only 𝑡 = 0 qualifies. If 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) ≥ 𝑘, then 𝐾 𝐿 > 𝑘 for every 𝑡 < 𝜆, so only 𝑡 = 𝜆
qualifies. Otherwise 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) < 𝑘 < 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋, and the intermediate value theorem gives exactly one
interior root. The equation 𝜋𝑏/[𝑏 + (1 − 𝜋)𝑡] = 𝑘/(𝜌Δ𝑇 ) gives the formula. Under rule I0 with
𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚(𝜋) ≤ 𝑘 < 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜋, the rate 𝑡 = 0 fails because 𝐾 𝐿 > 𝑘 there; the rate 𝑡 = 𝜆 fails because
𝐾 𝐿 ≤ 𝑘 there; and an interior rate requires rule IM.                                               □

Lemma A.5 (Product structure; analytical). Fix 𝑟 and 𝜋 with 𝜋 < 𝜏(𝑟), and rates with 𝑠 ≥ 𝑡. Then
𝜇0 (𝑠, 𝑡) ≤ 𝜋 < 𝜏. The tuple with the rates (𝑠, 𝑡) is an equilibrium if and only if these four conditions
hold:

  (i) (𝑠, 𝑒(1)) is a pair of Lemma A.3;

  (ii) 𝑡 is a rate of Lemma A.4;

 (iii) 𝑒(0) = 𝑒(−1) = 𝜌;

 (iv) the prices are 𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑡0 + 𝑒(𝑥) 𝑤(𝜇(𝑥)), and 𝜇(𝑃(𝑥))
                                                   ¯       = 𝜇(𝑥).

Proof. If 𝑠 ≥ 𝑡, then 𝑏 0 + 𝜆 − 𝑠 ≤ 𝑏 0 + 𝜆 − 𝑡, so the odds of 𝜇0 are at most 𝜋/(1 − 𝜋). Hence
𝜇0 ≤ 𝜋 < 𝜏.
    Necessity. In any equilibrium, Lemma 2(d) gives 𝑒(0) = 𝜌 at the posterior 𝜇0 < 𝜏, and
Lemma 2(b)–(c) give condition (iv). Lemma 3(d) shows that (E5) for class 𝐻 involves only
(𝑠, 𝜇(1), 𝑒(1)), and (E5) for class 𝐿 only (𝑡, 𝜇(−1), 𝑒(−1)). So (E5) gives (i) and (ii).
    Sufficiency. Take rates and entry that satisfy (i) to (iv), and check each condition by direct
computation. (E1): the posteriors are computed from 𝑓𝐻 and 𝑓 𝐿 . (E2): the stated entry is 𝜌 at
the posteriors 𝜇− (𝑡) and 𝜇0 , both at most 𝜋 < 𝜏, and it is 𝑒(1) ≥ 𝜌 at 𝜇+ (𝑠) ≥ 𝜋, with 𝑒(1) = 1
when 𝜇+ (𝑠) > 𝜏. So entry lies in [𝜌, 1] and is nondecreasing in the posterior. By the paragraph on
constructed tuples in Appendix A.2, orders with distinct posteriors have distinct prices, and orders
with equal posteriors have equal entry and equal prices. Hence the orders with one price share
one posterior, and (E2) gives 𝜇(𝑃(𝑥))
                                  ¯        = 𝜇(𝑥). (E3): at each price the belief is a posterior of an
order, and the stated entry is the one that Lemma 2(d) allows at that posterior under the tie rule in
use; condition (i) states it at the order 1, and the posteriors 𝜇− (𝑡) and 𝜇0 lie below 𝜏. (E4): (A.3)
with the stated entry. (E5): by Lemma 3(d), the payoffs of class 𝐻 involve only (𝑠, 𝜇(1), 𝑒(1)),
and those of class 𝐿 only (𝑡, 𝜇(−1), 𝑒(−1)); conditions (i) and (ii) state optimality for each. (E6):
Appendix A.1.                                                                                        □

The equilibrium set at the prior 1/2

At 𝜋 = 1/2, 1 − 𝜇+ (𝑠) = 𝑏/(2𝑏 + 𝑠), 𝜇− (𝑡) = 𝑏/(2𝑏 + 𝑡) and 𝑠∗ = 𝑏(2𝜏 − 1)/(1 − 𝜏). Five forms of
the high class’s pair appear. In each, the low class uses the rate of Lemma A.4, which at 𝜋 = 1/2 is

   𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟) = 0 on [𝑟 0 , 𝑟 𝑁 ],   𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟) = 𝑏 [𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/𝑘 − 2] on (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ),      𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟) = 𝜆 on [𝑟 𝑆 , 𝑟 2 ].

   • N (no informed trade): 𝑠 = 0, 𝑒(1) = 𝜌.

   • R (a mixed purchase that recruits): 𝑠 = 𝑠 𝑅 (𝑟) := 𝑏[Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/𝑘 − 2], 𝑒(1) = 1.

   • F (a full purchase): 𝑠 = 𝜆; 𝑒(1) = 1 for 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟𝐶 , and 𝑒(1) = 𝜌 for 𝑟 > 𝑟𝐶 .

   • Q (mixed orders at the entry floor): 𝑠 = 𝑠𝑄 (𝑟) := 𝑏[𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)/𝑘 − 2] = 𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟), 𝑒(1) = 𝜌.

   • T (the threshold pair): 𝑠 = 𝑠∗ (𝑟), 𝑒(1) = 𝑒 ∗ (𝑟) = 𝑘/𝐽 (𝑟).

Each form defines the equilibrium tuple completely:

   • orders: 𝜎𝐻 (1) = 𝑠/𝜆, 𝜎𝐻 (0) = 1 − 𝑠/𝜆, 𝜎𝐿 (−1) = 𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟)/𝜆, 𝜎𝐿 (0) = 1 − 𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟)/𝜆;

   • posteriors: 𝜇− (𝑡 𝐿 ), 𝜇0 (𝑠, 𝑡 𝐿 ) and 𝜇+ (𝑠) after the orders −1, 0 and 1;

   • challenger beliefs: 𝜇(𝑃(𝑥))
                         ¯       = 𝜇(𝑥) at each price;

   • high-cost preparation: 𝜁 (𝑃(1)) = (𝑒(1) − 𝜌)/(1 − 𝜌), and 𝜁 (𝑃(𝑥)) = 0 at the prices of the
     orders 0 and −1;

   • prices: 𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑡0 + 𝑒(𝑥) 𝑤(𝜇(𝑥));

   • bids: truthful.

If two orders share a price, they share the posterior and the entry (Lemma A.5), so 𝜁 is well defined
as a function of the price.

Proof of Proposition 3. Fix any slot in the band and the prior 1/2. The proof has eight steps.
    Step 1: the frame. By Lemma A.1(a), 𝜏(𝑟) ≥ 𝜏(𝑟 0 ) > 1/2 = 𝜋 on the path, so Lemmas A.3
to A.5 apply at every 𝑟. Every equilibrium has a high-class pair from Lemma A.3 and a low-class
rate from Lemma A.4. Step 4 shows 𝑠 ≥ 𝑡 for every such combination. Lemma A.5 then gives the
equilibrium set as their product. Recall 1 − 𝑀 = 1/(2 + 𝑢) and 𝑢𝑏 = 𝜆.
    Step 2: the high-class pairs. Use Lemma A.1, the definitions of the thresholds, and Lemma A.2.

   • (N) holds if and only if 𝜌Δ𝑇 /2 ≤ 𝑘, that is 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟 𝑁 .


                                                      59
   • (R): Δ𝑇 𝑏/(2𝑏 + 𝑠) = 𝑘 gives 𝑠 = 𝑠 𝑅 (𝑟). Then 𝜇+ (𝑠 𝑅 ) > 𝜏 if and only if 𝑘/Δ𝑇 < 1 − 𝜏, that is
     𝐽 (𝑟) > 𝑘, that is 𝑟 > 𝑟 𝐴 . Also 𝑠 𝑅 < 𝜆 if and only if Δ𝑇 /𝑘 < 2 + 𝑢, that is Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀) < 𝑘, that
     is 𝑟 < 𝑟 𝐹 . So (R) holds if and only if 𝑟 𝐴 < 𝑟 < 𝑟 𝐹 , under rule IM.

   • (Q): 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑏/(2𝑏 + 𝑠) = 𝑘 gives 𝑠 = 𝑠𝑄 (𝑟). Then 𝑠𝑄 > 0 if and only if 𝑟 > 𝑟 𝑁 ; 𝜇+ (𝑠𝑄 ) < 𝜏 if
     and only if 𝜌𝐽 (𝑟) < 𝑘, that is 𝑟 < 𝑟 𝐽 ; and 𝑠𝑄 < 𝜆 if and only if 𝜌Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀) < 𝑘, that is 𝑟 < 𝑟 𝑆 .
     Since 𝑟 𝐽 < 𝑟 𝑆 , (Q) holds if and only if 𝑟 𝑁 < 𝑟 < 𝑟 𝐽 , under rule IM.

   • (T): 𝑠∗ ∈ (0, 𝜆) if and only if 1/2 < 𝜏(𝑟) < 𝑀, that is 𝑟 < 𝑟𝐶 . Also 𝜌 ≤ 𝑒 ∗ ≤ 1 if and only if
     𝜌𝐽 (𝑟) ≤ 𝑘 ≤ 𝐽 (𝑟), that is 𝑟 𝐴 ≤ 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟 𝐽 , and 𝑒 ∗ = 1 if and only if 𝑟 = 𝑟 𝐴 . So (T) holds at 𝑟 𝐴
     under rule C1, and on [𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝐽 ] under rule CM, under rule IM in both cases.

   • (F): for 𝑟 < 𝑟𝐶 , 𝑀 > 𝜏(𝑟), so 𝑒(1) = 1, and the condition is Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀) ≥ 𝑘, that is 𝑟 ≥ 𝑟 𝐹
     (𝑟 > 𝑟 𝐹 under rule I0). For 𝑟 > 𝑟𝐶 , 𝑀 < 𝜏(𝑟), so 𝑒(1) = 𝜌, and 𝜌Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀) ≥ 𝑘 holds because
     𝑟 > 𝑟𝐶 > 𝑟 𝑆 . At 𝑟 = 𝑟𝐶 , rule C1 gives 𝑒(1) = 1; rule CM allows any 𝑒(1) ∈ [𝜌, 1], and every
     such value satisfies the condition because 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟𝐶 )(1 − 𝑀) > 𝑘. So (F) holds if and only if
     𝑟 ≥ 𝑟 𝐹 (rule IM) or 𝑟 > 𝑟 𝐹 (rule I0).

    Step 3: the low-class rate. At 𝜋 = 1/2, Lemma A.4 gives 𝑡 = 0 if and only if 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑡 = 𝜆 if
and only if 𝑟 ≥ 𝑟 𝑆 , and 𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟) = 𝑏(𝜌Δ𝑇 /𝑘 − 2) on (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ), under rule IM. Under rule I0 no rate
qualifies if and only if 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚 ≤ 𝑘 < 𝜌Δ𝑇 /2, that is on (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ].
    Step 4: 𝑠 ≥ 𝑡. For 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑡 = 0 ≤ 𝑠. For 𝑟 > 𝑟 𝑁 , the pairs are F with 𝑠 = 𝜆 ≥ 𝑡; Q with
𝑠 = 𝑠𝑄 (𝑟) = 𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟); and T with 𝑟 𝑁 < 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟 𝐽 . For T, 𝑠∗ ≥ 𝑡 𝐿 (𝑟) if and only if (2𝜏 − 1)/(1 − 𝜏) ≥
𝜌Δ𝑇 /𝑘 − 2, that is 1/(1 − 𝜏) ≥ 𝜌Δ𝑇 /𝑘, that is 𝜌𝐽 (𝑟) ≤ 𝑘, which holds for 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟 𝐽 . By Lemma A.5,
entry after the zero order and after a sale is 𝜌 in every equilibrium.
    Step 5: the set. Combine Steps 2 to 4 with the order of Lemma A.2. Under rules IM and C1 the

on (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝐽 ); F on [𝑟 𝐽 , 𝑟 2 ]. Under rule CM, T also exists on (𝑟 𝐴 , 𝑟 𝐽 ], and at 𝑟𝐶 entry after a purchase
in F can be any 𝑒(1) ∈ [𝜌, 1]. Under rule I0, (Q), (R) and (T) drop out, (F) needs 𝑟 > 𝑟 𝐹 , and Step 3
removes every equilibrium on (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ]. The set under rule I0 is N on [𝑟 0 , 𝑟 𝐹 ]; N and F on (𝑟 𝐹 , 𝑟 𝑁 ];
empty on (𝑟 𝑁 , 𝑟 𝑆 ]; and F on (𝑟 𝑆 , 𝑟 2 ].
    Step 6: verification of each tuple. Fix a form and a strength at which Steps 2 and 3 admit it. Step

by direct computation. In particular, if two orders have equal posteriors, one of them is 0 or −1, so
the common posterior is at most 1/2 < 𝜏, and both orders have entry 𝜌 and the same price.

with the four cases of the corollary. The proof has three steps.
     Step 1: strengths with 𝜋 < 𝜏(𝑟). Lemmas A.3 to A.5 apply. No informed trade is an equilibrium
if and only if (N) holds in both lemmas, that is 𝜌Δ𝑇 max{𝜋, 1 − 𝜋} ≤ 𝑘; then 𝜇0 = 𝜋 < 𝜏 and

𝑘 ≤ 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇+ (𝑠)] ≤ Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝜏) = 𝐽 (𝑟), so 𝑟 ≥ 𝑟 𝐴 by Lemma A.1(b).
     Conversely, let 𝜏(𝑟) ≤ 𝑀 (𝜋) and 𝑟 ≥ 𝑟 𝐴 , so 𝐽 (𝑟) ≥ 𝑘. If Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀 (𝜋)) ≥ 𝑘, pair (F) holds with
𝑒(1) = 1. Otherwise 𝜏 < 𝑀 (𝜋), and 𝑠 ↦→ Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇+ (𝑠)] is continuous and strictly decreasing on
[𝑠∗ , 𝜆], from 𝐽 (𝑟) ≥ 𝑘 to Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀 (𝜋)) < 𝑘. It equals 𝑘 at exactly one 𝑠 ∈ [𝑠∗ , 𝜆). At 𝑠 = 𝑠∗ this is
(T) with 𝐽 (𝑟) = 𝑘 and 𝑒(1) = 1; at 𝑠 > 𝑠∗ it is (R). With 𝑡 = 0 ≤ 𝑠, Lemma A.5 completes the tuple.
So a strength with 𝜋 < 𝜏(𝑟) lies in 𝐶 (𝜋) if and only if it satisfies the three conditions in the display.

    Step 2: strengths with 𝜋 ≥ 𝜏(𝑟). Under rule C1, no informed trade gives the posterior
𝜋 ≥ 𝜏 after every order, so 𝑒 ≡ 1. It is an equilibrium if and only if Δ𝑇 max{𝜋, 1 − 𝜋} ≤ 𝑘.
Suppose this holds, and take any candidate with 𝑠 > 0. Then 𝜇+ (𝑠) > 𝜋 ≥ 𝜏, so 𝑒(1) = 1 and

for every prior; at the benchmark 3/4 − 𝜏𝐴 = 0.109261.

Lemma A.8 (Positive research needs a strict full purchase; analytical). Let 𝜏(𝑟) > 1/2, and take
an equilibrium with 𝜆ˆ > 0. Then 𝑈𝐻 (1) > 0 and 𝜎𝐻 (1) = 1. Also 𝜇(1) = 𝑀𝜆ˆ , 𝜇(0) ≤ 1/2,
𝜇(−1) ≤ 1/2, and 𝜁 (𝑃(0)) = 𝜁 (𝑃(−1)) = 0.

Proof. By Lemma A.6, 𝜆ˆ = min{𝜆, ¯ 𝑉/𝑎} > 0, so 𝑉 > 0. By Lemma A.7(c) and 𝑈 𝑗 (0) = 0, either
𝑈𝐻 (1) > 0 or 𝑈 𝐿 (−1) > 0. Suppose 𝑈 𝐿 (−1) > 0. Then the sale is the unique maximizer of
class 𝐿, and (E5) gives 𝜎𝐿 (−1) = 1. With 𝜎𝐻 (−1) = 0, 𝑓𝐻 (−1) = 𝑏ˆ and 𝑓 𝐿 (−1) = 𝑏ˆ + 𝜆, ˆ so
𝜇(−1) = 𝑚 𝜆ˆ ≤ 1/2 < 𝜏(𝑟). Hence 𝑒(−1) = 𝜌 and 𝑈 𝐿 (−1) = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚 𝜆ˆ − 𝑘 > 0. The purchase then
earns 𝑈𝐻 (1) = 𝑒(1)Δ𝑇 [1 − 𝜇(1)] − 𝑘 ≥ 𝜌Δ𝑇 (1 − 𝑀𝜆ˆ ) − 𝑘 = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝑚 𝜆ˆ − 𝑘 > 0, by Lemma A.7(a).

under both investor rules. Lemma A.7(d) gives the three posteriors. Since 𝜏(𝑟) > 1/2, Lemma 2(d)
gives 𝜁 (𝑃(0)) = 𝜁 (𝑃(−1)) = 0 under both challenger rules.                                   □

A.6    Robustness
This subsection states and proves Proposition A.3 and the results that Section 6 summarizes. Each
proof uses the conventions of Appendix A.2.

The fixed-information control

Proposition A.3 (Fixed information; analytical). Fix the information experiment. It consists of
the joint law of the class and a signal 𝑆 that the challenger observes, the class value distributions,
and the independent cost law. Let the value depend on 𝑆 only through the class. If the incumbent
strengthens from 𝐹0 to 𝐹1 , entry does not rise under rule C1. Its decrease is exactly
                          {︁                              }︁
            E0 − E1 = Pr 𝐵 𝐹1 (𝜇(𝑆)) < 𝐶 ≤ 𝐵 𝐹0 (𝜇(𝑆)) ,          𝜇(𝑆) = Pr( 𝑗 = 𝐻 | 𝑆),

Proof of Proposition A.3. The cost is independent of the class, the values and the signal 𝑆, so
𝜇(𝑆) = Pr( 𝑗 = 𝐻 | 𝑆) is the challenger’s complete class belief at its decision. The value depends on
𝑆 only through the class, so gross profit in economy 𝑖 is 𝐵 𝐹𝑖 (𝜇(𝑆)). Under rule C1, the challenger
prepares exactly when 𝐶 ≤ 𝐵 𝐹𝑖 (𝜇(𝑆)). Proposition 1(a) gives 𝐵 𝐹1 (𝜇) ≤ 𝐵 𝐹0 (𝜇) at every belief.
Hence, pointwise,

         1{𝐶 ≤ 𝐵 𝐹0 (𝜇(𝑆))} − 1{𝐶 ≤ 𝐵 𝐹1 (𝜇(𝑆))} = 1{𝐵 𝐹1 (𝜇(𝑆)) < 𝐶 ≤ 𝐵 𝐹0 (𝜇(𝑆))} ≥ 0.

The joint law of (𝑆, 𝐶) is the same in both economies. Take expectations: E0 − E1 equals the
probability in the proposition, which is nonnegative, and it is positive exactly when the decrease
is strict. A tie probability 𝑞 that is the same in both economies replaces each indicator by
1{𝐶 < 𝐵 𝐹𝑖 } + 𝑞 1{𝐶 = 𝐵 𝐹𝑖 }. With 𝐵 𝐹1 ≤ 𝐵 𝐹0 , the difference of the two expressions is 0 for 𝐶 < 𝐵 𝐹1
and for 𝐶 > 𝐵 𝐹0 , 1 − 𝑞 at 𝐶 = 𝐵 𝐹1 < 𝐵 𝐹0 , 1 strictly between, 𝑞 at 𝐶 = 𝐵 𝐹0 > 𝐵 𝐹1 , and 0 when the
two profits are equal; it is nonnegative, so entry does not rise with such a tie probability either. □

The two-point cost law of Section 2.2 is not needed. Let the preparation cost 𝐶 be independent of

Proposition A.4 (Preparation cost laws; analytical). Let the classes satisfy Assumption 1, and let 𝐹1
strengthen 𝐹0 . Suppose

bounds on every profile, and agrees with every value.

Complementary private signals

A buyer may know its integration plans, while investors who follow the target know its customers
or its product market. Two conditionally independent binary signals represent the two sources.
The investor observes 𝑇 ∈ {+, −} with accuracy 𝑎𝑇 , and the challenger observes 𝑌 ∈ {+, −} with
accuracy 𝑑:

         Pr(𝑇 = + | 𝐻) = Pr(𝑇 = − | 𝐿) = 𝑎𝑇 ,             Pr(𝑌 = + | 𝐻) = Pr(𝑌 = − | 𝐿) = 𝑑,

with 1/2 < 𝑎𝑇 , 𝑑 < 1. The investor orders on its signal, and the challenger sees the price and its
signal before it decides. Write 𝑚 = 𝑚(1/2), 𝑀 = 𝑀 (1/2), 𝜇− = (1 − 𝑎𝑇 ) + (2𝑎𝑇 − 1)𝑚 and 𝜇+ =
(1−𝑎𝑇 ) + (2𝑎𝑇 −1)𝑀. A challenger with signal + holds the belief 𝜙+ (𝜇) = 𝑑𝜇/[𝑑𝜇 + (1− 𝑑)(1− 𝜇)].
Write 𝐷 + (𝑟) = Δ𝑇 (𝑟) + (1 − 𝜌)(2𝑑 − 1) [𝑡 𝐻 (𝑟) − 𝑡0 (𝑟)] for the largest price response to the public

Each mixed tuple is unique under rule IM with either challenger rule, by the strict monotonicity
of each return and the endpoint signs. Under rule I0 neither strength has an equilibrium, because
the unique admissible rates are interior. Without the challenger’s signal, entry is 𝜌 under every
profile at both strengths, zero orders are forced at 𝑟 0 , and at 𝑟 1 the unique rates are 𝑠 = 𝑡 = 4𝑏/3;
this control also needs rule IM, since the gross return at zero, 0.033333333, exceeds 𝑘 and the
return at full orders, 0.017929428, falls short of it. The rational constants above are exact; the script
audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/P4_information/derive.py prints them
with every tuple, and audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/XP4/xp4_check.py
enumerates every rate on each order side and finds no other equilibrium.


A.7     Welfare and sale terms
This subsection states and proves Propositions A.8, A.9 and A.10, and it proves the statements of
Section 7 that rest on them.

Access to prices

Hold the incumbent at 𝐹1 . Compare the equilibrium of Proposition 2 with the equilibrium of an
economy in which the challenger does not see the price. All other primitives stay fixed, and market
makers set competitive prices in both economies. Write R for expected target proceeds and W for
expected acquisition surplus net of preparation costs. The superscript 1 marks price access, and 0
marks the hidden price. Acquisition surplus excludes transfers between bidders, shareholders and
traders.

Proposition A.8 (Access to prices; analytical). Let the hypotheses of Proposition 2 hold. At 𝐹1 , both
economies have unique full orders. Price access raises expected target proceeds and acquisition

surplus net of preparation costs:

                 R 1 − R 0 = (1 − 𝜌) 𝜋(𝑏 + 𝜆)(𝑡 𝐻 − 𝑡 0 ) + (1 − 𝜋) 𝑏 (𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡 0 ) > 0,
                                    {︁                                            }︁

                W 1 − W 0 = (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜋𝜆) 𝐵1 (𝑀) − 𝑐 𝐻 + 𝑝𝐹1 ( 𝑝) > 0.

Proof of Proposition A.8. The two tuples. The access tuple is E1 of the proof of Proposition 2. The
hidden tuple has full orders, the posteriors (𝑚, 𝜋, 𝑀) at the orders, the challenger belief 𝜋 at every
price because the challenger sees neither the price nor the order, 𝜁 ≡ 0, entry 𝜌 after every order, the
prices 𝑃0 (𝑥) = 𝑡 0 + 𝜌 𝑤(𝜇(𝑥)), and truthful bids. Here and below every object is at 𝐹1 .
     Verification of the hidden tuple. (E1): the full-order likelihoods. (E2): the challenger’s belief
is its prior. (E3): 𝐵1 (𝜋) ≤ 𝐵0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 , strictly. (E4): (A.3) with 𝑒 ≡ 𝜌. (E5): with 𝑒 ≡ 𝜌, the

economy, so its orders are unique, and the tuple is unique. The access economy is unique by
Proposition 2.
     The proceeds gain. Couple the two economies on the class, the value, the cost, the slot identity
and the liquidity order. The orders coincide at every realization, so the trading costs coincide.
Access adds high-cost preparation exactly after the order 1, which has probability 𝑏 + 𝜆 in class 𝐻
and 𝑏 in class 𝐿. With entry, expected proceeds rise from 𝑡0 to 𝑡 𝑗 . Hence

                         R 1 − R 0 = (1 − 𝜌){𝜋(𝑏 + 𝜆)𝑤 𝐻 + (1 − 𝜋) 𝑏 𝑤 𝐿 }.

Each term is nonnegative, and 𝑤 𝐻 = 𝑤 𝐿 + Δ1 ≥ Δ1 > 0, so the gain is positive.
    The surplus gain. Let V0 (𝑅) = 𝑅1{𝑅 ≥ 𝑝} be the allocation value without the challenger. With
truthful bids, the target goes to the highest admissible value, so with an admissible challenger of
value 𝜃 the allocation value is max{V0 (𝑅), 𝜃}, and

                   max{V0 (𝑅), 𝜃} − V0 (𝑅) = (𝜃 − max{𝑝, 𝑅})+ + 𝑝1{𝑅 < 𝑝}.                        (A.9)

For 𝑅 < 𝑝, both sides equal 𝜃, since 𝜃 ≥ 𝑝; for 𝑅 ≥ 𝑝, both sides equal (𝜃 − 𝑅)+ . Average over 𝑅
and over the class at the posterior 𝑀 of the order 1: one added preparation adds 𝐵1 (𝑀) + 𝑝𝐹1 ( 𝑝)
to allocation value and costs 𝑐 𝐻 . Added preparations have probability (1 − 𝜌) Pr(𝑋 = 1) =
(1 − 𝜌) (𝑏 + 𝜋𝜆). Hence W 1 − W 0 = (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜋𝜆) [𝐵1 (𝑀) − 𝑐 𝐻 + 𝑝𝐹1 ( 𝑝)] > 0, because
𝐵1 (𝑀) > 𝑐 𝐻 . Transfers between bidders, shareholders and traders cancel from this sum.         □

The benchmark, the transfer and the trade scale. At 𝑟 1 , proceeds are 0.872531 with access and

Access outside the recruitment region. Suppose instead 𝐵1 (𝑚) < 𝑐 𝐻 < 𝐵1 (𝜋) and 𝑘 < 𝜌𝜅Δ1 .

The payment rule

Consider a verifiable-value institution with zero reserve. After diligence, values are verifiable, and a
sale to the next buyer at its value is an enforceable fallback. The seller and the winner divide the
surplus above the fallback by Nash bargaining, with seller weight 𝜔 ∈ [0, 1). Without a challenger,
the seller receives 𝜔𝑅.

Proposition A.9 (The payment rule; analytical). Let the challenger’s values be the point masses ℓ
and ℎ, and let the incumbent distribution have support [0, 𝑟]
                                                           ¯ with 0 < ℓ < 𝑟¯ < ℎ. The transfer, the

                                                  87
challenger’s expected profit and the spread are

                           𝑇𝜔 (𝑅, 𝜃) = (1 − 𝜔) min{𝑅, 𝜃} + 𝜔 max{𝑅, 𝜃},
                                𝑔𝜃,𝜔 = (1 − 𝜔) E[(𝜃 − 𝑅)+ ],
                                 Δ𝜔 = 𝜔(ℎ − ℓ) + (1 − 2𝜔) E[(𝑅 − ℓ)+ ].

A stronger incumbent weakly lowers the challenger’s profit. Its effect on the spread weakly has the
sign of 1 − 2𝜔, and strictly when E[(𝑅 − ℓ)+ ] changes strictly.

Proof of Proposition A.9. The continuation. After preparation, the buyer with the largest value
acquires the target, and the next buyer’s value 𝑧 is the enforceable fallback; without a challenger, the
fallback is zero. Let the winner’s value be 𝑉. For 𝑉 > 𝑧 and 0 < 𝜔 < 1, Nash bargaining maximizes
(𝑃 − 𝑧) 𝜔 (𝑉 − 𝑃) 1−𝜔 over 𝑧 ≤ 𝑃 ≤ 𝑉. The logarithm has derivative 𝜔/(𝑃 − 𝑧) − (1 − 𝜔)/(𝑉 − 𝑃)
and a strictly negative second derivative, so the unique optimum solves 𝜔(𝑉 − 𝑃) = (1 − 𝜔)(𝑃 − 𝑧),
that is 𝑃 = (1 − 𝜔)𝑧 + 𝜔𝑉. At 𝑉 = 𝑧, feasibility fixes 𝑃 = 𝑧, and at 𝜔 = 0 the next buyer accepts the
fallback, so 𝑃 = 𝑧. Hence the transfer is 𝑇𝜔 (𝑅, 𝜃) = (1 − 𝜔) min{𝑅, 𝜃} + 𝜔 max{𝑅, 𝜃}.
    Profit and spread. A challenger wins when 𝜃 > 𝑅 and keeps 𝜃 − 𝑇𝜔 = (1 − 𝜔)(𝜃 − 𝑅), so
𝑔𝜃,𝜔 = (1 − 𝜔)E[(𝜃 − 𝑅)+ ]. For 𝑅 ≤ ℓ, both classes win, and 𝑇𝜔 (𝑅, ℎ) − 𝑇𝜔 (𝑅, ℓ) = 𝜔(ℎ − ℓ). For
ℓ < 𝑅 < ℎ, the high class wins and the low class loses, and 𝑇𝜔 (𝑅, ℎ) − 𝑇𝜔 (𝑅, ℓ) = (1 − 𝜔)𝑅 + 𝜔ℎ −
(1 − 𝜔)ℓ − 𝜔𝑅 = 𝜔(ℎ − ℓ) + (1 − 2𝜔)(𝑅 − ℓ). The support lies below ℎ, so the average over the
two cases gives Δ𝜔 = 𝜔(ℎ − ℓ) + (1 − 2𝜔)E[(𝑅 − ℓ)+ ].
    Signs. The function (𝜃 − 𝑅)+ is nonincreasing in 𝑅, and (𝑅 − ℓ)+ is nondecreasing. A first-
order stochastic strengthening of the incumbent therefore weakly lowers 𝑔𝜃,𝜔 and weakly moves
E[(𝑅 − ℓ)+ ] up, so the spread moves with the sign of 1 − 2𝜔, strictly when E[(𝑅 − ℓ)+ ] rises strictly
and 𝜔 ≠ 1/2.                                                                                           □

    For 𝑅 ∼ 𝑈 [0, 𝑟], E[(𝑅 − ℓ)+ ] = (𝑟 − ℓ) 2 /(2𝑟) = Δ𝑇 (𝑟), so Δ𝜔 (𝑟) = 𝜔(ℎ − ℓ) + (1 − 2𝜔)Δ𝑇 (𝑟).
In the benchmark, Δ𝜔 (𝑟 1 ) − Δ𝜔 (𝑟 0 ) = (1 − 2𝜔) [Δ𝑇 (𝑟 1 ) − Δ𝑇 (𝑟 0 )] = 13/20 (1 − 2𝜔), which is zero
exactly at 𝜔 = 1/2. This result specifies the acquisition stage after every history; the trading and
preparation equilibrium under this rule is outside the proposition.

Proposition A.10 (Reserves; analytical). Fix a reserve, and allow class values below it. Keep the
class order, the costs, the slot and a strengthening 𝐹1 ≤ 𝐹0 , and compute each class object with the
reserve. Suppose 𝑘 < 𝜌𝜅 min{Δ0 , Δ1 }.

 (a) If 𝐵0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 < 𝐵1 (𝑀), entry is the same at the two strengths: E0 = E1 = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜋𝜆).

 (b) If 𝐵0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 < 𝐵0 (𝑀) and 𝐵1 (𝑀) < 𝑐 𝐻 , entry falls strictly: E0 = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)(𝑏 + 𝜋𝜆) >
     𝜌 = E1 .

Each economy has a unique outcome under every pair of tie rules.

Proof of Proposition A.10. Orders. The zero-cost challenger prepares after every price, so entry is
at least 𝜌. Lemma 3 uses only (E1), (E4), 𝑒 ∈ [𝜌, 1] and Δ𝑇 ≥ 0, so it holds with the reserve kernel.

     Entry. Gross profit falls with incumbent strength at every belief, because (𝜃 − max{𝑝, 𝑅})+ is
nonincreasing in 𝑅. In part (a), 𝐵1 (𝜋) ≤ 𝐵0 (𝜋) < 𝑐 𝐻 < 𝐵1 (𝑀) ≤ 𝐵0 (𝑀), so at both strengths only
the order 1 recruits, and 𝐵𝑖 (𝑚) ≤ 𝐵𝑖 (𝜋) keeps the sale below the cost. In part (b), the weak strength
has the same pattern, and at the strong strength 𝐵1 (𝜇) ≤ 𝐵1 (𝑀) < 𝑐 𝐻 at every posterior. Every
realized comparison is strict.
     The tuples. At each strength the tuple has full orders, posteriors (𝑚, 𝜋, 𝑀), entry (𝜌, 𝜌, 1) or
(𝜌, 𝜌, 𝜌) as above, 𝜁 (𝑃(𝑥)) = [𝑒(𝑥) − 𝜌]/(1 − 𝜌), prices 𝑃𝑖 (𝑥) = 𝑡 0,𝑖 + 𝑒𝑖 (𝑥) 𝑤 𝑖 (𝜇(𝑥)), 𝜇(𝑃

𝜇(𝑥), and truthful bids. Since Δ𝑖 > 0 and entry is nondecreasing in the posterior, the three prices

      margin there is 0.050792. At 𝑝 = ℎ with point values, every acquisition profit is zero, the

these results.


A.8     Empirical implications
This subsection states and proves the results that Section 8 summarizes. The auction is the
benchmark, the prior is 1/2, and the slot lies in the band of Proposition A.1(a). The price increment
of entry 𝐴𝑟 (𝜇) = 𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡0 + Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝜇 is the function 𝑤(𝜇) of Appendix A.2 at the strength 𝑟. It is
positive, rises in 𝜇 with slope Δ𝑇 , and rises in 𝑟, because 𝑡 𝐿 − 𝑡0 = 12 − 1/(8𝑟) and Δ𝑇 rise in
𝑟 at the benchmark. The indicator 𝐽𝑟 = 1{𝐵𝑟 (𝑀) ≥ 𝑐 𝐻 } records whether a purchase recruits,
and the volume 𝑣 = Pr(𝑋 ≠ 0) is the frequency of nonzero orders per order opportunity. Write
𝑑 = 𝑏 + 𝜆/2, 𝜗 = 𝑢/(2 + 𝑢) = 2𝑀 − 1 = 𝑀 − 𝑚, and 𝑒 + = 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 . At the benchmark,
𝐵𝑟 (1/2) = 5 − 𝑟/4 + 1/(8𝑟) < 𝑐 𝐻 for 1 < 𝑟 < ℎ. The top profit 𝐵𝑟 (𝑀) falls strictly in 𝑟 for 𝑟 > ℓ,
equals 9𝑀 + 3/8 at 𝑟 = ℓ, and lies below 𝑐 𝐻 at 𝑟 = ℎ; so for 𝑀 > 5/8 the ceiling 𝑟𝐶 is the unique
strength at which 𝐵𝑟 (𝑀) = 𝑐 𝐻 , and 𝐽𝑟 = 1 exactly when 𝑟 ≤ 𝑟𝐶 . In the band, 𝑀 − 5/8 = 0.106059
at the benchmark slot.

Full two-sided trade on (𝑟 𝑆 , ℎ). The strength 𝑟 𝑆 solves 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝑚 = 𝑘 exactly: 𝑟 𝑆 = 𝔯(𝐷 𝑆 ) with
𝐷 𝑆 = 𝑘/(𝜌𝑚) = 0.297463. For 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 < ℎ, every candidate satisfies

                 𝐾 𝐻 ≥ 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)(1 − 𝑀) = 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝑚 > 𝑘,            𝐾 𝐿 ≥ 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝑚 > 𝑘

by (A.5), since 1 − 𝑀 = 𝑚 at the prior 1/2 and Δ𝑇 rises. So full orders are the unique equilibrium

unit purchase and a unit sale. The purchase impact is 𝐼+ = 𝑃(1) − 𝑃(0), and the sale impact is
𝐼− = 𝑃(0) − 𝑃(−1). The model has no separate quote stage: each price is the competitive value
conditional on the order.

Proposition A.11 (The spread and its entry component; analytical). Let 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 < ℎ.

 (i) 𝑆 = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗 + (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀): a class component and an entry component.

(ii) 𝐼− = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗/2 and 𝐼+ = 𝐼− + (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀). Hence 𝐼+ > 𝐼− if and only if a purchase recruits.

(iii) If the challenger does not see the price, full two-sided trade is still the unique outcome, and 𝑆
      equals the class component 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗.

(iv) On (𝑟 𝑆 , 𝑟𝐶 ], both components, both impacts and 𝑆 rise strictly with 𝑟. On (𝑟𝐶 , ℎ), the entry
     component is zero, and the class component, both impacts and 𝑆 rise strictly.

 (v) At 𝑟𝐶 , 𝑆 falls by (1 − 𝜌) 𝐴𝑟𝐶 (𝑀) > 0. The prices 𝑃(−1) and 𝑃(0) and the sale impact are
     continuous there; 𝑃(1) and the purchase impact fall by this amount.

Proof of Proposition A.11. Let 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 < ℎ, so the outcome is F .

 (i) Subtract the prices: 𝑆 = 𝜌[ 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀) − 𝐴𝑟 (𝑚)] + (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀). Since 𝐴𝑟 has slope Δ𝑇 ,
     𝐴𝑟 (𝑀) − 𝐴𝑟 (𝑚) = Δ𝑇 (𝑀 − 𝑚) = Δ𝑇 𝜗.

(ii) Also 𝐴𝑟 (1/2) − 𝐴𝑟 (𝑚) = Δ𝑇 (1/2 − 𝑚) = Δ𝑇 𝜗/2 and 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀) − 𝐴𝑟 (1/2) = Δ𝑇 𝜗/2, so
     𝐼− = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗/2 and 𝐼+ = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗/2 + (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀). Since 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀) > 0, 𝐼+ > 𝐼− exactly when
     𝐽𝑟 = 1.


                                                   92
(iii) With a hidden price the outcome is F with entry 𝜌 at every order, so part (i) holds with 𝐽𝑟
      replaced by 0.

(iv) On (𝑟 𝑆 , 𝑟𝐶 ], 𝐽𝑟 = 1; Δ𝑇 and 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀) rise strictly in 𝑟, and 𝜌, 𝜗 and 1 − 𝜌 do not depend on 𝑟. So
     both components, both impacts and 𝑆 rise strictly. On (𝑟𝐶 , ℎ), 𝐽𝑟 = 0: the entry component is
     identically zero, and the class component 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗, the impacts 𝐼− = 𝐼+ = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗/2 and 𝑆 = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜗
     rise strictly.

(v) Since 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟𝐶 , both sides of 𝑟𝐶 lie in F . Rule C1 gives 𝐽𝑟𝐶 = 1, and 𝐽𝑟 = 0 for 𝑟 > 𝑟𝐶 . Every
    other term is continuous in 𝑟. So 𝑃(−1), 𝑃(0) and 𝐼− are continuous at 𝑟𝐶 , and 𝑃(1), 𝐼+ and 𝑆
    fall by (1 − 𝜌) 𝐴𝑟𝐶 (𝑀) > 0.                                                                    □

    In the benchmark at 𝑟 1 the class component is 0.077020 and the entry component 0.709279,
so 𝑆 = 0.786299; at 𝑟𝐶 the spread falls from 0.969913 to 0.108078. Under rule CM, entry after a
purchase at 𝑟𝐶 can take any value in [𝜌, 1], and 𝑆(𝑟𝐶 ) can take any value between its two one-sided
limits. The spread is the difference between conditional execution prices; the model has no separate
quote stage.

Proposition A.12 (Identification of the informed share; analytical). Let 𝑟 𝑆 < 𝑟 < ℎ, and write
𝑣 = 2𝑏 + 𝜆.

(a) Counts. The order law depends on (𝜆, 𝛽) only through 𝑣, and entry 𝜌 + (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝑣/2 depends
    on them only through 𝑣 and 𝐽𝑟 . For each ratio 𝑢′ > 0, exactly one pair (𝜆′, 𝛽′) with this ratio
    has the volume 𝑣.

(b) Incentives. A slot with informed share 𝜗′ produces these counts through full two-sided trade at
                   ¯
    𝑟 only if 𝜗′ ≤ 𝜗(𝑟) = 1 − 2𝑘/(𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)).

 (c) Band. The slots with strict recruitment at 𝑟 1 and strict exclusion at 𝑟 2 have informed shares
     exactly in (2𝜏(𝑟 1 ) − 1, 2𝜏(𝑟 2 ) − 1). At the benchmark volume, each share in this band belongs
     to a slot with the same counts and the same entry at 𝑟 1 and at 𝑟 2 . Full two-sided trade is the
     unique outcome of this slot at both strengths.

(d) Prices. Given the entry floor 𝜌 = Pr(preparation | 𝑋 ≠ 1) and the spread Δ𝑇 (𝑟), the sale impact
    and the volume identify the slot: 𝜗 = 2𝐼− /(𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)), 𝜆 = 𝑣𝜗 and 𝛽 = 𝑣(1 − 𝜗)/[2(1 − 𝑣𝜗)].

 (e) Purchases only. When only class 𝐻 trades, the counts identify the slot: 𝜆 = 2E[𝑋] and
     𝑏 = (𝑣 − E[𝑋])/2.

Proposition A.13 (Orders predict the winner; analytical). In the point-mass benchmark:

 (i) 𝑤 𝑥 = 𝜇(𝑥) + [1 − 𝜇(𝑥)] ℓ/𝑟, and Pr(𝑊 | 𝑋 = 𝑥) = 𝑒(𝑥) 𝑤 𝑥 .

(ii) Under full two-sided trade, 𝑤 1 − 𝑤 −1 = 𝜗(1 − ℓ/𝑟), and this gap rises strictly with 𝑟.

(iii) Under full two-sided trade, E[𝑋] = 0 and Cov(𝑋, 1 𝐴 ) = (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 (𝑏 + 𝜆/2). For 𝑟 > 𝑟𝐶 ,
      Cov(𝑋, 1 𝐴 ) = 0 and Cov(𝑋, 1𝑊 ) = (𝑏 + 𝜆/2) 𝜌 𝜗 (1 − ℓ/𝑟) > 0.



                                                     94
Proof of Proposition A.13. (i) The cost is independent of the class and of 𝑅, so preparation leaves
    the class posterior unchanged: Pr(𝐻 | 𝑋 = 𝑥, 𝐴) = 𝜇(𝑥). A prepared high-class challenger
    wins, because ℎ > 𝑟. A prepared low-class challenger wins exactly when 𝑅 < ℓ, with probability
    ℓ/𝑟. Hence 𝑤 𝑥 = 𝜇(𝑥) + [1 − 𝜇(𝑥)]ℓ/𝑟, and Pr(𝑊 | 𝑋 = 𝑥) = 𝑒(𝑥)𝑤 𝑥 .

(ii) In F , 𝑤 1 − 𝑤 −1 = (𝑀 − 𝑚)(1 − ℓ/𝑟) = 𝜗(1 − ℓ/𝑟), and its derivative in 𝑟 is 𝜗ℓ/𝑟 2 > 0. The
     function 𝜇 ↦→ 𝜇 + (1 − 𝜇)ℓ/𝑟 rises strictly, and the prices of F rise strictly with the posterior,
     so the win probability of a prepared challenger rises with the price.

(iii) In F , E[𝑋] = 0, so for any indicator 𝑌 with conditional means 𝑦 𝑥 = E[𝑌 | 𝑋 = 𝑥],
      Cov(𝑋, 𝑌 ) = E[𝑋𝑌 ] = 𝑑 (𝑦 1 − 𝑦 −1 ). For 𝑌 = 1 𝐴 , 𝑦 1 − 𝑦 −1 = 𝑒 + − 𝜌 = (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 , so
      Cov(𝑋, 1 𝐴 ) = (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝑑. For 𝑌 = 1𝑊 , 𝑦 𝑥 = 𝑒(𝑥)𝑤 𝑥 . For 𝑟 > 𝑟𝐶 , 𝑒 ≡ 𝜌, so Cov(𝑋, 1 𝐴 ) = 0
      and Cov(𝑋, 1𝑊 ) = 𝑑𝜌(𝑤 1 − 𝑤 −1 ) = 𝑑𝜌𝜗(1 − ℓ/𝑟) > 0.                                          □

    Add a public event after the price that reveals only whether the challenger prepared, and nothing
else. Market makers then price again.

Proposition A.14 (Announcement returns; analytical). After the order 𝑥:

 (i) the price changes by Δ1𝑥 = [1 − 𝑒(𝑥)] 𝐴𝑟 (𝜇(𝑥)) when the event reports preparation, and by
     Δ0𝑥 = −𝑒(𝑥) 𝐴𝑟 (𝜇(𝑥)) when it reports no preparation, an event of positive probability exactly
     when 𝑒(𝑥) < 1;

(ii) when 𝑒(𝑥) < 1, the mean change over both reports, with their model probabilities, is zero;

(iii) the mean change in a sample of preparation reports alone is positive;

(iv) under full two-sided trade, the price after no order lies (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 (𝑏 + 𝜆/2) 𝐴𝑟 (𝑀) below the
     expected price before the order opportunity, although the posterior after no order is 1/2.

Proof of Proposition A.14. After the order 𝑥, the event reveals whether the challenger prepared.

 (i) The cost is independent of the class, so Pr(𝐻 | 𝑥, prepared) = 𝜇(𝑥), and with preparation
     E[𝑉𝑇 ] = 𝑡 𝑗 in class 𝑗. So the price after a report of preparation is 𝑡 𝐿 + Δ𝑇 𝜇(𝑥) = 𝑡 0 + 𝐴𝑟 (𝜇(𝑥)).
     Without preparation E[𝑉𝑇 ] = 𝑡 0 in both classes. Subtract 𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑡0 + 𝑒(𝑥) 𝐴𝑟 (𝜇(𝑥)): Δ1𝑥 =
     [1 − 𝑒(𝑥)] 𝐴𝑟 (𝜇(𝑥)) and Δ0𝑥 = −𝑒(𝑥) 𝐴𝑟 (𝜇(𝑥)). The report of no preparation has probability
     1 − 𝑒(𝑥), positive exactly when 𝑒(𝑥) < 1.

(ii) When 𝑒(𝑥) < 1, 𝑒(𝑥)Δ1𝑥 + [1 − 𝑒(𝑥)]Δ0𝑥 = 𝑒(𝑥) [1 − 𝑒(𝑥)] 𝐴𝑟 − [1 − 𝑒(𝑥)]𝑒(𝑥) 𝐴𝑟 = 0.

      term is nonnegative, because 𝐴𝑟 > 0, and the term of an order with 𝑒(𝑥) < 1 is positive. In F ,
      𝑒(−1) = 𝜌 < 1, so the mean is positive.

(iv) In F , the expected price before the order opportunity is 𝑃¯ = 𝑥 Pr(𝑋 = 𝑥)𝑃(𝑥) = 𝑡0 +

     𝜌[𝑑𝐴𝑟 (𝑚) + (1− 2𝑑) 𝐴𝑟 (1/2) + 𝑑𝐴𝑟 (𝑀)] + (1− 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝑑𝐴𝑟 (𝑀). Since 𝐴𝑟 is affine and 𝑚 + 𝑀 = 1,
     𝑑𝐴𝑟 (𝑚) + 𝑑𝐴𝑟 (𝑀) = 2𝑑𝐴𝑟 (1/2), so 𝑃¯ = 𝑡0 + 𝜌 𝐴𝑟 (1/2) + (1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝑑𝐴𝑟 (𝑀). The price after
     no order is 𝑡 0 + 𝜌 𝐴𝑟 (1/2), so 𝑃(0) − 𝑃¯ = −(1 − 𝜌)𝐽𝑟 𝑑𝐴𝑟 (𝑀), while 𝜇(0) = 1/2.         □

    In the benchmark at 𝑟 1 , the expected price is 0.872531 and the price after no order is 0.614583.
A sample of preparation reports has mean change 0.160592 at 𝑟 1 and 0.701042 at 𝑟 2 . The zero mean
of part (ii) needs both reports with their model probabilities.

Two clusters at intermediate strengths. Let 𝑟 𝐹 < 𝑟 < 𝑟 𝑁 , so 𝑟 < 𝑟𝐶 . Proposition 3 gives exactly
two equilibria under rules IM and C1: N, and F with 𝜎𝐿 = 𝛿0 (purchases only). In the second,
Pr(𝑋 = 1) = 𝑏 + 𝜆/2 and Pr(𝑋 = −1) = 𝑏, so E[𝑋] = 𝜆/2 = 0.168061. The posterior after a sale
is 1/2, and after no order it is 𝜇0 = (1 − 2𝑏 − 𝜆)/(2 − 4𝑏 − 𝜆), with 1/2 − 𝜇0 = 𝜆/[4(1 − 𝑣𝑈 )] > 0,
𝑣𝑈 = 2𝑏 + 𝜆/2. Both orders have entry 𝜌, so 𝑃(−1) − 𝑃(0) = 𝜌Δ𝑇 𝜆/[4(1 − 𝑣𝑈 )] > 0; at 𝑟 = 1.60
it is 0.005363. After a purchase every challenger prepares. In N the three prices coincide and entry
after a purchase is 𝜌. Under rule CM, the threshold pair T of Proposition 3 adds a third tuple: at
𝑟 = 1.60 class 𝐻 buys with probability 0.492845, entry after a purchase is 0.506106, the mean order
is 0.082828, the low-class sale earns −0.005938, and entry is 0.321311. The exhaustive two-cluster
statement therefore uses rule C1.

Certificates. All values of this subsection come from the script audit/slot_manuscript_2026_
10_23/scripts/P5_observables/p5_observables.py. It computes every value in interval
arithmetic at 60 digits from the exact input 𝛽 = 294655/106 and an enclosure of e; a sign counts only
when the enclosure excludes zero. The independent program audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_
23/scripts/XP5/recompute.py (run log audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/
XP5/run_log.txt) rebuilds every value from the auction cash flows and the slot probabilities,
with a rational Taylor bound on e through the term 1/120!, and it records one comparison row per
value in audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/XP5/registry_recompute.csv.

                𝜌 = 0.85,         𝑐 𝐻 = 7.14,         𝑘 = 0.015,          𝑟 0 = 1.1,       𝑟 1 = 2.3,

and the accuracies (𝑎𝑇 , 𝑑) = (0.70, 0.75), each with status input. Its primitive margins are positive:

of strengths within 𝑆(𝐵); they do not describe calibration B.

script in its last column; the codes are

Every margin that depends on e is computed in outward interval or exact rational interval arithmetic,
and its sign is certified only when the whole enclosure lies on one side of zero. The independent
recomputations in audit/slot_manuscript_2026_10_23/scripts/XP1/, XP2/, XP3/, XP4/
and XP5/ of the same folder reproduce every display; XP1/xp1_registry_recompute.csv and
XP3/exact_enclosures.json record exact rational endpoints. The structural bound 𝛽 ≤ 1/3 is a
domain restriction; the table records its distance. Exact ties, such as 𝑟 = 𝑟𝐶 or 𝑢 = 𝑢 lo , are boundary
cases outside the open sets.

Open parameter sets. Each block of Table A.6 lists strict inequalities between continuous
functions of the primitives, so each defines an open set of primitives that contains the benchmark;
with the structural bound 𝛽 ≤ 1/3, the set is relatively open in the model domain. The sections
through the benchmark are: the slot band and the prior range of Proposition A.1 (Table 2); the cap
strip and the cost window of Proposition A.2; the support conditions of Proposition A.4, which
allow every half-width below 0.002688902 for all three parts; the reserve intervals of Appendix A.7;
and, for Section 8, the set 0 < 𝛽 < 1/3 with the slot band and 𝑟 𝑆 (𝑀) < 𝑟 < ℎ. On the last set the
uniqueness margin 𝜌Δ𝑇 (𝑟)𝑚 − 𝑘 tends to zero as 𝑟 ↓ 𝑟 𝑆 , so no single positive margin covers the
whole interval.
